May had thought of it. Just for a moment she saw herself at the ball, dancing, floating in the arms of a tall stranger with a fascinating ugly face﻿—

Not being able to have a new dress wouldn’t matter﻿—she could fix up the *eau de Nil* corded silk with the coffee stain on the skirt that Aunt Priscilla had given them, and that had been too grand for anything they would be apt to go to. She was so slender she could take that front breadth right out. And she had seen just the trimming for it in Wilmington﻿—bands of tiny shells. Rainbow-colored shells peeping from waves of faint water-green silk﻿—a mermaid come from the foam.

“*I have been looking for you all my life. Where have you been hidden away?*”

“Where? Where?” the violins cried. “Where?” cried the plucked strings of the harp.

“*But now that I have found you﻿—*”

===Chinese===
玛吉把维克托的礼服挂上晾衣绳，好散一散樟脑味。他要上纽约去，住爸爸当年住过的第五大道酒店（Fifth Avenue Hotel），去参加露西·霍桑的舞会。姑娘们也接到过一张上了亮光的米色请帖，莉莉却由它随手摊在门厅桌上，摆在人人一眼瞧得见的地方；可玛吉说了，她们当然是想也不用想的。

梅倒是想过。有那么一刹那，她看见自己置身舞会，在一个高个子陌生男人的臂弯里旋转、飘荡——那男人有一张丑得迷人的脸——

做不起新衣裳也不要紧——普丽西拉婶婶送的那条尼罗水绿（*eau de Nil*）棱纹绸裙可以拾掇出来，就是裙摆上带一块咖啡渍的那条；那料子太贵气，她们往后能去的场合没有一个压得住它。她身条那么纤细，索性把前幅整幅抽去就是。缀边她也在威尔明顿相中了——一道道小贝壳的绲边。彩虹色的小贝壳，从淡淡水绿的丝绸波浪里探出头来——一位从泡沫里走出来的美人鱼。

“*我找了你一辈子了。你一直被藏在哪儿？*”

“哪儿？哪儿？”小提琴哀声问。“哪儿？”竖琴拨动的弦哀声问。

“*可如今我总算找到了你——*”

===Original===
But, of course, she knew that they couldn’t afford it. They really couldn’t afford it for Victor, but for him they would manage somehow.

Coming back from the clothesline, Maggie stopped by the bed of lilies-of-the-valley by the porch, such a convenient place for cherry stones. How sweet, how sweet! She never could get by them, even when she had a hundred things to do in the house she must pause before their altar. Now there was Victor’s grey suit to sponge and press for the trip, a cake to make, and lunch to get, but she knelt beside them, breathing their fragrance, loving them. She gathered a spray or two of great white bells, their stems squeaking as she pulled them, and a cool green leaf, to take into the kitchen with her. They had never been so beautiful.

Why shouldn’t Victor take a big bunch of them to Lucy? She suggested it to him when he came strolling out to the kitchen where she was cracking eggs for an omelet.

“I’ll pack them in a nice box with wet cotton, and you take them up just as soon as you get there﻿—Victor, *don’t* eat those raisins, you’ll spoil your lunch. You’ll see they haven’t anything like these in New York.”

“Oh, well, I guess they’ll have plenty of flowers﻿—I don’t believe I’d bother﻿—I mean, I guess they’ll have enough,” said Victor, dreadfully embarrassed.

“They won’t have any like these, and it won’t be a bit of trouble. I’ll pack the box right in your bag,” Maggie said, killing a currant on the table with great firmness under the impression that it was a fly; and she thought, “If he takes them in the afternoon, they’ll know he’s reached New York, and they’ll certainly ask him to supper, even if it’s just a pickup meal because of the party.”

===Chinese===
当然，她心里明白，这钱她们花不起。就是为维克托置办，她们其实也花不起，可为了他，总归能想出个法子来。

从晾衣绳那边回来，玛吉在门廊旁那畦铃兰跟前停住了脚——这地方，吐樱桃核再顺手不过。多香啊，多香啊！她从来没法打它们旁边径直走过，哪怕屋里有一百件事催着，她也必得先在它们的圣坛前流连片刻。这会儿，维克托旅途穿的那身灰西装要刷要熨，蛋糕要做，午饭要备，她却跪在花旁，呼吸着它们的香气，一心一意地疼它们。她采下一两串大白铃铛——掐茎的时候，茎秆吱地一声轻响——又摘下一片凉沁沁的绿叶，一并带进厨房去。它们从来没有这样美过。

怎么不让维克托带一大束去送给露西呢？他踱进厨房来的时候——她正磕着鸡蛋预备摊蛋卷——她就跟他说了。

“我把它们装进一只漂亮盒子，垫上湿棉花，你一到就赶紧给人家送去——维克托，*不许*吃那些葡萄干，要吃坏午饭的。你等着瞧吧，纽约可没有这样的花。”

“哦，这个，我想他们那儿花多得很——我看就不必费事了——我是说，我想他们花够多的了。”维克托说，窘得要命。

“他们绝没有这样的花，再说一点儿也不费事。我把盒子直接装进你的提包。”玛吉说着，果断地一巴掌拍死桌上一颗葡萄干——只当它是只苍蝇；心里还想：“要是他下午就把花送到，人家就知道他到纽约了，必定会留他吃晚饭，哪怕因为开舞会，只是顿便饭。”

===Original===
Oh, she did want him to have a good time! Her heart still ached so for him, because he had not been able to go back to Harvard. She had suffered for him all winter﻿—so hard on him hanging around the place. She would look at him standing in the window watching the falling snow, whistling and rocking back and forth from his toes to his heels, or yawning by the fire over “A Daughter of Heth,” or “Just As I Am,” and think that really he would be happier doing some sort of work. But Victor felt that it was important to wait until just the right position offered itself.

Maggie found a splendid box for the lilies-of-the-valley, but just because it said “1 pr. Corsets, extra heavy boning” Victor was ridiculous, even though she offered to paste a piece of plain paper over the shocking words. So they had to be put in another box, not nearly as good.

He would much rather have let them stay at the hotel, much much rather. But he thought of Maggie going out last night after she had finished the dishes﻿—she wouldn’t let May wash them because of her pretty hands, or Lily, because she broke too many plates﻿—gathering a great burst of them, carillons of silver bells, cool with evening, and bringing them in for him to see and smell, her face shining.

===Chinese===
哦，她多盼着他玩得痛快！她心里还为他疼得慌呢，因为他没能回哈佛去。整个冬天她都替他受着罪——一个大男人成天在家里晃荡，多难熬啊。她常常看着他站在窗前望下雪，打着呼哨，脚尖点地脚跟落地地来回摇晃，或是守着炉火打哈欠，面前摊着《赫丝之女》（“A Daughter of Heth”），要么是《我今如此》（“Just As I Am”），心里想，他真去做点儿什么事，兴许倒快活些。可维克托觉得，非得等到恰好合适的位子自己送上门来不可——这才是顶要紧的。

玛吉找到一只装铃兰再好不过的盒子，可就因为盒盖上印着“1 pr. Corsets, extra heavy boning”（紧身胸衣一副，特别加骨），维克托就嫌它寒碜，哪怕她主动提出拿一张素纸把那几个不像话的字糊上也不成。结果只好另换一只盒子，差得远了。

他宁可把花留在旅馆里，非常非常宁可。可他想起昨天晚上玛吉洗完碗碟后还出门去——洗碗的活儿她不让梅沾手，为着她那双好看的手；也不让莉莉，为着她打碎的盘子太多——采下一大捧铃兰，一捧银钟，浸透了晚间的凉气，捧进屋来给他看、给他闻，脸庞放着光。

===Original===
He meant just to leave them at the big brownstone house. He was astonished to hear himself asking the butler, “Is Miss Hawthorn in?”

“Is that the man about the extra chairs?”

Mrs. Hawthorn came into the hall, all sweeping violet silk and foaming lace﻿—was she dressed for the ball already at five in the afternoon, or could it be only a tea-gown?

“Are you the man about the extra chairs?”

“Oh﻿—I’m Victor Campion, Mrs. Hawthorn﻿—I just﻿—I wondered if Lucy﻿—”

“Oh. Mr. Campion. Yes, I remember.” She gave him a finger and said in a preoccupied voice, “I’m sorry, Lucy is lying down.”

“Oh, that’s all right! I just brought a few lilies-of-the-valley, they aren’t anything really, but we thought﻿—”

He followed her into the drawing-room, cleared of most of its furniture, and sat on the edge of a small gilt chair, not wanting to stay, but afraid she would be hurt if he went right away. He felt his face getting red. “It’s warm today, isn’t it?” he asked, laughing a little, nervously.

“Heavens, will the goose *never* go?” thought Mrs. Hawthorn; and she let a moment pass before she answered:

“Very warm.”

He had never seen such a grand parlor. The high sky-blue ceiling was painted with clouds and cupids, and from it hung gas globes like giants’ eggcups, with the profiles of Roman emperors clear on the ground glass. Long mirrors reflected over and over again the firescreen of terra-cotta silk embroidered with bullrushes, the marble lady looking down at a butterfly perched on her shoulder, Mrs. Hawthorn’s violet draperies, and his own best grey suit and flaming face.

Would it be all right to go now? He didn’t know how late you could get supper at the hotel, and he felt as if he had been sitting there for hours. There was a clock on the mantelpiece, but it was so fancy, its hands were such delicate traceries of golden frost-work against a golden moon of background that he couldn’t read it at all.

“Well, I think maybe I’d better﻿—”

And somehow he was out of the house, on the street. What a relief!

===Chinese===
他本打算只把花撂在那座褐石大宅就算了。却听见自己开口问管家：“霍桑小姐在家吗？”连他自己都吃了一惊。

“是来办加椅子那回事的人吗？”

霍桑太太走进门厅，一身紫罗兰绸裙曳地，花边翻涌如沫——才下午五点，她是已经穿上了舞会的衣裳呢，还是说那不过是件家常茶袍？

“你就是来办加椅子那回事的？”

“哦——我是维克托·坎皮恩，霍桑太太——我只是——我想问问露西——”

“哦，坎皮恩先生。是的，我记得。”她伸出一根手指头给他握，心不在焉地说：“对不起，露西正躺着呢。”

“哦，那没关系！我只是带了几枝铃兰来，实在算不得什么，不过我们想着——”

他跟着她进了客厅——家具已搬走了大半——在一张小镀金椅子的边沿上坐下；他不想多待，又怕立时起身告辞会伤她的心。他觉出自己的脸红起来了。“今天真暖和，是不是？”他问，不大自然地干笑了一声。

“天哪，这只呆鹅*到底*走不走哇？”霍桑太太心里想；她让一刻工夫空了过去，才答话：

“是很暖和。”

他从没见过这么气派的客厅。高高的天蓝色穹顶上画着云朵和丘比特，垂下来几盏煤气灯，灯罩像巨人的蛋杯，磨砂玻璃上罗马皇帝的侧脸清清楚楚。一面面长镜，一遍又一遍地映着那架绣了香蒲的陶土红绸防火屏、那位低头看肩头蝴蝶的大理石淑女、霍桑太太紫罗兰色的裙裾，还有他自己那身最好的灰西装和烧得通红的脸。

这就走，合不合规矩呢？他不知道旅馆的晚饭能开到多晚，只觉得自己仿佛已经在这儿坐了几个钟头。壁炉架上倒是有一座钟，可它太花哨了，钟针是金色的霜花细纹，衬着金月亮似的钟面，他压根儿认不出几点。

“呃，我想也许我该——”

不知怎么一来，他已经出了宅门，站到街上去了。多松快啊！

===Original===
“*Mercy!*” cried Mrs. Hawthorn, flapping her hands with exasperation, as she hurried into the dining-room for a last look at the table before the florist’s men left. All white and palest green, lilies-of-the-valley, masses and masses of them.

Lucy, rosy with sleep, came trailing down, wrapped in a dressing gown of pale blue silk trimmed with swansdown, like fluffy white clouds in a summer sky, and helped herself to a glacé pear from a dish on the table.

“Your rustic admirer has been here,” said her mother. “That Campion young man. He left a box of something or other for you somewhere, and he hopes we’ll understand why his sisters couldn’t come. Lucy Hawthorn, those extra chairs aren’t here *yet*!”

“Oh, Mother! To think of Victor’s coming all the way here for my ball! Oh, *can’t* we ask him to the dinner?”

Her flower-blue eyes filled with tears as she thought of his lonely dinner at the hotel, her hand went out for a piece of crystallized pineapple.

“No we can’t﻿—there really isn’t room for him. I’m sorry, but good gracious! Who would have *dreamed* of the goose coming all that distance? Now, Lucy, don’t cry, or you’ll spoil your eyes, and don’t eat any more candied fruit, or you’ll spoil the looks of the table.”

===Chinese===
“*老天爷！*”霍桑太太喊道，气恼地直拍双手——她正赶进饭厅去，趁花店的伙计还没走，最后看一眼桌子。满眼的白，配上最淡的绿，铃兰，一丛又一丛，堆得望不到边。

露西睡得两颊绯红，拖拖拉拉地下楼来，裹一件镶天鹅绒边的淡蓝绸晨衣，像夏日天空里一团团毛茸茸的白云，自己动手从桌上碟子里拣了颗糖渍梨。

“你那位乡下爱慕者来过了。”她母亲说。“就是坎皮恩家那个小伙子。他把一盒什么玩意儿撂在哪儿给你了，还盼着我们多多包涵，体谅他的妹妹们为什么没能来。露西·霍桑，那批加的椅子到这会儿*还*没到呢！”

“哦，妈妈！想想看，维克托大老远地赶到这儿来，就为了我的舞会！哦，咱们*不能*请他来吃顿晚饭吗？”

想到他将孤零零一个人在旅馆里吃晚饭，她那双花儿般蓝的眼睛盈满了泪水；她那只手却又伸出去，摸了一块糖霜菠萝。

“不能，就是不能——真匀不出他的位子了。我很抱歉，可是老天爷！谁*想*得到这只呆鹅会跑那么老远的路？好了露西，别哭了，要把眼睛哭坏的；也别再吃糖渍果子了，要把这一桌的体面吃坏的。”

---

===Original===
Should he dress before dinner, or after? He couldn’t decide, and this time he couldn’t call over the stairs and ask one of the girls. If he could only go down and look first, to see what other people did. Perhaps, if he got dressed up, he’d be too conspicuous.

And for one weak moment he thought, “I don’t believe I want any dinner. They’ll be sure to have refreshments at the Hawthorns’.” But he did go down.

Everything on the menu in French! He knew what some of the words meant, and he could have pronounced them splendidly to the girls, but pronouncing them to the waiter who had been fairly sputtering French to another waiter was different.

“Er﻿—how is this today?”

The man stopped humming a little song up above Victor’s head, and asked meanly, ignoring his pointing finger:

“How is what, sir?”

How purposeful and efficient the other diners looked, how perfectly clear about everything. He finished his first course, moved his glass to hide a spot of gravy on the table cloth, and waited for his chocolate ice-cream.

He waited and waited. Other people who had come in after him finished their dinner and went. He wouldn’t stand it! He frowned, drumming on the table, and looked at his watch. Well, he had a long, long time before he need leave the hotel, but still he wasn’t going to sit there and be ignored. He tried to see his waiter among those who skated past with bowls of salad or tureens of soup, who set down the dishes with a flourish that was almost a caress.

===Chinese===
礼服该在晚饭前换，还是晚饭后换？他拿不定主意，这一回又没法隔着楼梯喊哪个妹妹来问。要是能先下去瞧一瞧别人怎么做就好了。若是穿戴得齐齐整整地下去，兴许太扎眼了。

有那么一个没出息的瞬间，他想：“我看晚饭也不用吃了。霍桑家肯定备着茶点呢。”可他到底还是下去了。

菜单上全是法文！有几个词他认得，念给姑娘们听，他能念得漂亮极了；可要念给侍者听——那侍者方才还对着另一位侍者噼里啪啦地讲法文——就是另一回事了。

“呃——这个，今天怎么样？”

那人停下了在维克托头顶上哼着的小曲，全不理会他指着的菜名，凉凉地问：

“什么怎么样，先生？”

别的用餐人个个看上去目标笃定、干净利落，桩桩件件都清清楚楚。他吃完了头一道，把杯子挪过去遮住桌布上一点肉汁渍，等着上他的巧克力冰淇淋。

他等啊，等啊。比他进来得晚的客人都吃完了晚饭走了。他再也受不了了！他皱起眉，手指敲着桌面，看了看表。好吧，离他该动身离开旅馆还早得很早得很，可他也犯不着坐在那儿遭人冷落。他想在那一溜端着沙拉盆、汤盅滑来滑去的侍者中间，找出自己那一个——他们放下菜来那一手，潇洒得近乎抚爱。

===Original===
He wouldn’t stand it another minute! And he said meekly to a waiter so young and inexperienced that he allowed his eye to be caught:

“Oh﻿—would you mind telling my waiter I’m ready for my ice-cream now?”

And all of a sudden, for the first time, he really felt, he really believed that in two or three hours he was going to see Lucy. He could feel his heart thumping; he was dizzy, dreamy with happiness.

“Anything more, sir?”

“Oh-a﻿—what?”

He must have eaten his ice-cream without ever noticing it, lost in thoughts of Lucy, for there was the saucer in front of him, with just a few pale brown streaks left in it.

===Chinese===
他一分钟也忍不下去了！于是他低声下气地跟一个侍者搭话——那小子年轻没经验，才容他递了个眼色：

“哦——劳驾，可否请你告诉我的那位侍者，我这就等着上冰淇淋了？”

冷不丁地，头一回，他真切地感到、真切地相信：再过两三个钟头，他就要见到露西了。他听得见自己的心咚咚地撞；他晕晕乎乎，幸福得如在梦里。

“还要点别的吗，先生？”

“哦——啊——什么？”

他准是想露西想得出了神，冰淇淋吃下去都浑然不觉——瞧，碟子就摆在面前，只剩下几道浅褐色的痕子了。

===Original===
He was almost on the Hawthorns’ steps before he decided that he was too early. So he took a long walk; and, when he came back, music was pouring from the open windows, carriages were rolling up and away, and a small crowd was watching gentlemen in crush hats and ladies in swansdown *sortie des bals* skim over the scarlet carpet from carriage to door. He followed them in more haughtily than any king.

Out of the blur, among all the people to whom he gave his beaming smile and dazzled blue gaze, he saw only Lucy﻿—Lucy with her silky hair and apple-blossom skin, wearing a blue satin gown hand-painted with daisies, with a tight little basque buttoned down the front and a great, long grownup train pouring out from masses of puffs and drapings.

The pianist flung up his hands and scattered a shower of silver drops. Flute, harp, and violin awoke; and at last Victor and Lucy were dancing together.

*One*, two, three, and a *one*, two, three!

He wished it had been a waltz﻿—there was something so brisk about a polka. And what a crowd! Bump! “Excuse me, Lucy!” Bump! “Excuse me!”

Flowers everywhere﻿—roses, carnations, heliotrope. And masses and masses of lilies-of-the-valley﻿—his were a drop in the sea, lost.

===Chinese===
差不多已到霍桑家的台阶跟前，他才认定自己来得太早了。于是他遛了一大圈；等回来时，音乐正从敞开的窗口里涌出来，马车一辆辆驶到又驶走，一小群人围观着戴折叠礼帽的先生们和披天鹅绒*舞会披肩*（*sortie des bals*）的太太们掠过猩红地毯，从车厢一路飘进门里。他尾随他们进了门，那份傲气，赛过任何一位国王。

人影幢幢里，他满面堆笑、蓝眼睛亮得晃人地朝众人望过去，眼里却只装得下一个露西——丝绸般柔光的头发，苹果花般的肌肤，穿一身手绘雏菊的蓝缎长裙，紧窄的小上衣一排扣子直扣到底，从一团团泡袖、一道道褶裥里，倾泻出一幅长大的、大人气的裙裾。

钢琴家双手向上一扬，撒落一天银珠。长笛、竖琴、小提琴一齐醒了；维克托和露西终于跳上了舞。

*一*、二、三，再来一个*一*、二、三！

他巴不得这是一支华尔兹——波尔卡这东西未免太急促了。人也真多！撞了一下！“对不起，露西！”又撞了一下！“失陪失陪！”

到处是花——玫瑰、康乃馨、天芥菜。还有一丛又一丛的铃兰——他送的那把不过是沧海一粟，早没影儿了。

===Original===
*Bump!*

“It’s such a jam, let’s go to the conservatory,” Lucy suggested a little breathlessly. “Doesn’t the fountain sound cool? Oh, Victor, thank you so much for the *beau*tiful flowers﻿—see, I’m wearing a little bunch of them, they’re so much bigger and sweeter than the ones that came from the florist’s!”

Her hand poised above his flowers among the laces at her bosom, touched them lightly as a hovering white butterfly. The darling! The darling! His heart swelled with gratitude and love.

“Why, they aren’t anything﻿—really! I just thought﻿—”

“Oh, dear, there’s the music﻿—I must fly!”

“Lucy﻿—how soon can I have another dance?”

“Oh, Victor, I’m *terribly* sorry, but﻿—”

“Oh, that’s all right!” he assured her, too quickly, too eagerly.

“But I’ll introduce you to some nice girls.”

“Damn some nice girls!” he wanted to shout. But what he said was:

“That’ll be fine.”

===Chinese===
*撞！*

“这儿挤死了，咱们去花房吧。”露西略带气喘地提议。“那喷泉听着多凉快呀？哦，维克托，多谢你送来这*漂*亮*的花儿——瞧，我身上别了一小束呢，它们比花店里来的那些，朵儿大得多，也香得多！”

她的手抬起来，悬在他送的花上——那花正别在她胸口的花边里——像一只盘旋的白蝴蝶，轻轻一点。心肝儿！心肝儿！他的心涨满了感激和爱。

“嗐，它们实在算不得什么——真的！我只是想着——”

“哎呀，音乐响了——我得飞啦！”

“露西——还要多久，我才能再跟你跳一支？”

“哦，维克托，我*太*抱歉了，可是——”

“哦，没关系！”他赶忙宽慰她，答得太快，太急切。

“不过我给你介绍几位好姑娘。”

“见鬼去吧，什么好姑娘！”他真想这么喊。可他嘴上说的是：

“那敢情好。”

===Original===
“But didn’t I meet you at dinner?” asked the first nice girl.

“At dinner?”

“Yes, here at Lucy’s dinner tonight?”

“Oh﻿—no! No, I couldn’t get here to dinner,” Victor assured her. “I was unavoidably detained.”

Bump!

“Suppose we go to the conservatory﻿—it’s lots too crowded for dancing,” said the nice girl. She stole a look at her new pink satin slippers﻿—he had danced all over them! She pulled up her gloves, settled her bangles, and unfurled her fan with the swansdown edge.

“Doesn’t the fountain sound cool, Mr.﻿—eh﻿—?”

He had never felt so forlorn and homesick in his life. The music rose and fell sadly﻿—a waltz, this time. Stay with me, my love, my love! No, I cannot stay.

He squeezed his hands in their new white kid gloves between his knees. “The fountain sounds cool, doesn’t it?” he asked.

===Chinese===
“咦，我不是在晚饭上见过您吗？”头一位好姑娘问。

“晚饭上？”

“是啊，今晚在露西家的晚饭上，不是吗？”

“哦——没有！没有，我赶不来赴宴，”维克托一口咬定，“我因故绊住了，实在分不开身。”

撞了一下！

“要不咱们去花房吧——这儿挤得根本没法跳舞。”好姑娘说。她偷偷瞟了一眼自己那双新的粉缎舞鞋——全叫他踩了个遍！她把手套往上抻了抻，理好镯子，唰地抖开那把镶天鹅绒边的扇子。

“这喷泉听着多凉快呀，这位先生是——呃——？”

他这辈子从没这么凄惶、这么想家过。音乐哀哀地起了又落——这一回是一支华尔兹。留在我身边吧，我的爱人，我的爱人！不，我不能停留。

他把戴着崭新白羊皮手套的两只手夹在膝盖中间，使劲夹着。“这喷泉听着是挺凉快的，是不是？”他问。

---

===Original===
Maggie had said, “Don’t come home the day after the dance﻿—stay two nights. As long as you’re in New York you might as well really see it. And if the Hawthorns ask you to spend a few days, just you do it!”

So no one was expecting him as he bumped up the porch steps with his bag the afternoon after the ball. The lilies-of-the-valley perfumed the air﻿—he was almost surprised that they were still in bloom, it seemed so long since he left home yesterday morning. How strange to see the house when he was supposed to be miles and miles away, looking just as it would have looked if at this minute he had still been in New York. May’s flower scissors and a litter of wet stems and leaves, Lily’s old shade hat with its muslin bow, Maggie’s muddy overshoes, and Maggie herself coming up from the chicken-yard.

“*Victor!*” And then the delight changed to lamenting. “Oh, I told the butcher not to stop today!”

At her cry May and Lily came bursting out of the house to welcome him, to ask him a hundred questions.

===Chinese===
玛吉原说过：“跳完舞别第二天就回家——住上两夜。既然到了纽约，索性好好见识见识。要是霍桑家留你住上几天，你就只管住下！”

于是舞会次日午后，他提着提包跨上门廊台阶时，谁也没料到是他。空气里浮着铃兰的香——它们竟然还开着，他几乎有些诧异，昨天早晨离家，恍如隔了许久许久。明明该在几十里开外的人，却亲眼看见这宅子——它那模样，和此刻他若仍身在纽约时它该有的模样，分毫不差。梅的花剪和一摊湿漉漉的花茎残叶，莉莉那顶缀细纱蝴蝶结的旧遮阳帽，玛吉沾泥的套鞋，还有玛吉本人，正从鸡圈那边走上来。

“*维克托！*”跟着，欢喜就变了哀叹。“哎呀，我交代过肉贩子今天不必来了！”

听她一喊，梅和莉莉从屋里一拥而出，迎他进门，七嘴八舌地问上一百个问题。

===Original===
“Have they an elegant house?”

“Didn’t they like the lilies-of-the-valley?”

“Did Lucy look nice? What did she wear?”

“Oh, white, I think, or blue﻿—something, anyway.”

“Well, I should *hope* so!”

“What was the entertainment? Just salad and ice-cream, or did you have lobster? Do tell us!”

And as he answered he became confident, self-possessed, a man of the world kindly amused at all this feminine flutter. They saw him the center of the ball, the master of ceremonies, the strong oak for those three ivies, Mr. and Mrs. Hawthorn and Miss Hawthorn, to cling to. He almost saw himself so, through their eyes.

===Chinese===
“他们家的宅子气派吗？”

“他们喜欢那些铃兰吗？”

“露西好看吗？她穿的什么？”

“哦，白的吧，我想，要不就是蓝的——反正总归是穿了点什么的。”

“哼，*但愿*是穿了！”

“都款待了些什么？光是沙拉和冰淇淋呢，还是上了龙虾？快说给我们听！”

他一面作答，一面变得从容自信、有恃无恐，成了一位见过世面的男子，好性儿地看着这一屋子女人的雀跃忙乱。在她们眼里，他是舞会的中心、主持全局的司仪、一棵可供霍桑先生、霍桑太太、霍桑小姐这三株常春藤依傍的大橡树。借着她们的眼睛，他自己也差不多看见了这个光景。

## XX / XX

===Original===
Twice Lucy came to visit the Leafs’, and each time Victor loved her more. He thought of her all the summer, all the autumn, all the winter. Well, not quite all. Not when he was enjoying biscuits like little puffs of summer cloud, with the golden honey from the row of beehives along the grape-house, nor when he was wishing Maggie wouldn’t have mutton hash so often. Not when he was scratching mosquito bites, or blowing on cold fingers to warm them, not when he was brushing his teeth, putting Rowland’s Macassar Oil where his mustache should be but wasn’t, or discovering on Market Street that his sock had a hole in its heel. Not when he and Pip Grant and Tommy Holly, heads together, were singing at the top of their lungs:

===Chinese===
露西来利夫家做客过两回，每来一回，维克托就把她爱得更深一层。整个夏天他想她，整个秋天他想她，整个冬天他还是想她。嗯，也不尽然。大嚼那种像夏日云朵小泡芙似的饼干、蘸着葡萄暖房旁一排蜂箱里金黄的蜂蜜时，他不想；暗暗盼着玛吉别这么三天两头做烩羊肉末时，他不想；挠蚊子叮的包、往冻僵的手指上呵气取暖时，不想；刷牙时，往本该蓄髭须却始终空空如也的地方抹罗兰马卡萨生发油（Rowland’s Macassar Oil）时，或者在市场街（Market Street）上发现自己的袜子后跟破了个洞时，也不想。至于跟皮普·格兰特（Pip Grant）和汤米·霍利（Tommy Holly）头碰着头、扯开嗓子拼命唱的时候，那就更不想了：

===Original===
> Miss Judy O’Connor lived frinst me
 

 And tinder lines to her I wrote,
 

 If you dare say one hard word agin her
 

 *I’ll*﻿—thread on the tail of your mush, mush, mush,
 

 toorily addy
 

 Mush, mush, mush, toorily aye﻿—’ ”

===Chinese===
> 朱迪·奥康纳小姐住在我家对过，
 

 给她写去的情话一封又一封，
 

 谁敢说她半句不好听，
 

 *我就*——踩住你那团糊糊的尾巴尖，糊啊糊，糊啊糊，
 

 图里利艾迪，
 

 糊啊糊，糊啊糊，图里利艾——”

===Original===
But as much as most of us do when we say with all our hearts to the beloved, “I think of you every minute.”
And with the lilies-of-the-valley Lucy came again to the Leafs’, but only to say goodbye before she went abroad, to make the Grand Tour.
Victor wrote to her.

===Chinese===
不过，他惦记她的分量，也就同咱们大多数人差不多——咱们满心赤诚地对心爱的人说「我时时刻刻都在想你」的时候，心里想的其实也就那么多。

待到铃兰再度开放，露西又来了利夫家一趟，却只是为了辞行——她要远赴国外，走一趟「大旅行」（Grand Tour）。

维克托给她写了信。

---

===Original===
> I wanted to tell you something while you were here, but I couldn’t. But now I can’t hold in any longer. I love you. I love you so much. Darling, darling Lucy, do you think you can ever love me? I come to you stained and scarred﻿—I’m not worthy of you, but no man could be that, for you are like an angel, so lovely and innocent and good, I want to cry when I think about you.
> It means asking you to wait for me, Lucy. I haven’t anything to offer you except all my love, but I have accepted a position in Wilmington with a real estate company﻿—it is rather a small beginning, but it is my belief there is a big future in it, and I’ll work so hard for you, Lucy! Perhaps, I ought to have written to Mr. Hawthorn first, but I couldn’t until I knew whether you could care for me a little. How I will watch the mail for your answer! It nearly kills me when I think that soon the ocean will be between us, but I can stand anything if you send me word before you go that there is any hope for me.
> Lucy﻿—when I think how lovely you were to me the night before you left here, I feel so happy and I do love you so! I will keep your precious little handkerchief forever, and ever, and ever. Do you remember the moonlight on the river, and the way the lilies-of-the-valley smelled? They are “lilies-of-the-valley” to other people, but they are always “Lucy’s flowers” to me. I enclose a spray to remind you of me when you are far away.
> I love you.
> Yours forever and ever,
 

 **Victor.**

===Chinese===
> 你在这儿的时候，我本想告诉你一件事，却没能开口。如今我再也憋不住了。我爱你。我这样爱你。亲爱的、亲爱的露西，你想过没有，你将来能不能也爱我？我带着一身污痕、满心疮痍来到你面前——我配不上你，可也没有哪个男人配得上，因为你像一位天使，这样可爱、天真、善良，我一想起你就想哭。
> 这意味着要请你等我，露西。除了我全部的爱，我没有什么可以献给你；不过我已经在威尔明顿一家房地产公司接受了职位——起步虽小，我却相信它前程远大，而且我要为你拼命地干，露西！按理说，我本该先写信给霍桑先生，可我做不到——在不知道你对我有没有一丝心意之前，我做不到。我会怎样眼巴巴地守着邮件等你的回信啊！一想到不久之后一片大洋就要横在我们中间，我几乎痛死；可只要你在临行前捎一句话给我，说我还有一线希望，我就什么都受得住。
> 露西——一想起你行前最后一晚对我那样温柔美好，我就觉得那样幸福，我是这样爱你！你那块珍贵的小手帕，我要永远、永远、永远地珍藏。你还记得河上的月光，记得铃兰的香气吗？在别人，它们是「铃兰」；在我，它们永远是「露西的花」。随信附上一枝，愿你在远方见花如见我。
> 我爱你。
> 永永远远属于你的，
 

 **维克托。**

---

===Original===
Lucy wrote to Victor.

===Chinese===
露西给维克托写了信。

===Original===
> I was so surprised by your letter, I never had an *idea* you felt that way about me. I feel very much touched, and I really do love you as if you were my brother, but we are both *much* too young to talk of anything else. For *goodness sake* don’t *think* of writing to Father, for ever so long anyway, he and Mother would *die*, as they still consider me a babe in arms.
> You must excuse me for not answering your kind letter before we sailed, but really I hadn’t a *minute*. Any *woman* would understand the hubbub and confusion of getting ready for a trip like this, the trips to the *modiste’s* and milliner’s, as Mother and I both discovered we literally hadn’t a stitch to our names, goodbyes to friends, etc., etc., etc., though I don’t suppose a *mere man* would!
> We had a delightful trip, though at first I suffered from *mal-du-pays* as I thought of home and friends﻿—perhaps one friend in particular, as I looked at the lights on the water, and thought of the way the moon shone on the river that last evening at the Leafs’. But I resolved to be “awfully jolly,” as a Mr. Thompson on board was always saying, and not shed a tear (I didn’t quite keep *that* resolution!) and I made lots of pleasant acquaintances.
> Well, it was *awful* at first! The ship went up and down so! But after two days it was lovely. I had my new blue sailor costume, and we saw a whale and a homeward bound vessel (I couldn’t keep back a little sigh as I looked at *that*, I wonder if you can guess why!) and the little whitecaps seemed to be frolicking about the bow of our stately ship﻿—indeed, the wind blew so one day that the sailors had to climb the rigging to reef sail. (Don’t I sound nautical?) I found life at sea made everyone very hungry, and ready to do full justice to the *four* meals they had on the ship, breakfast at half past eight, lunch at twelve, dinner at four, and supper at eight.
> There were some desperate flirtations on board, of course. Everyone said Mr. Thompson ought to be named Mr. *Spoony*, but he was full of fun and the life of the party. Then there was Mr. Baron, a languid young swell whom I had met at some balls in New York, so of course he felt in duty bound to come to life and honor me with his attention when he wasn’t asleep, which was *generally*. His mother and sister were with him, but Mrs. Baron was too overcome with *mal-du-mer* to make her appearance, and I did *not* care for Miss Baron, who was quite a flirt and apparently did not care much for ladies’ society. I think the gentlemen were much nicer than the ladies on the ship; and, by the time the trip was over, we felt as if we had known each other for years, for our vessel was a slow one﻿—as Mr. Thompson said, “An old donkey could go faster.” But the days slipped by like magic, although we were all so idle﻿—indeed, the gentlemen seemed to have left all thoughts of business on shore, and the abandon of the ladies was *complete*. Sometimes in the evenings we would have jolly games of “Ruth and Jacob” with everybody joining in, and sometimes just sit up in the bow of the boat, looking at the foam and trying to look into the future. I think my companions would have been surprised if they had known where my thoughts were leading me, Victor!
> And here we are in very nice, though rather dark, lodgings, in London! Just think! I can hardly believe it. It looks very dirty, and I know I will be terrified on the streets, and I suppose they will be full of those dreadful people Dickens writes about. The floor is going up and down, just like the deck of the good ship “Hiawatha”﻿—I hope it stops before this evening, when Mother and I are going to the theatre with Mr. Thompson to see Geneviéve Ward in a play called, I *think*, “Forget-me-not.” Mr. Baron has been here too, in lavender kid gloves, with a big bouquet (*already*!) to ask us to the opera tomorrow night, to hear Patti sing. Aren’t people kind, and ain’t it fun?
> This is the longest letter I ever wrote in my life. I put the spray of lilies-of-the-valley you sent me into my prayerbook, and guess where it just *happened* to fall﻿—in the marriage service, where the woman says “I will”!!!
> Your affectionate
 

 Lucy.

===Chinese===
> 你那封信让我好生吃惊，我*压根儿*没想到你对我存着这样的心思。我很感动，也真心实意地喜欢你，就像喜欢自己的亲哥哥一样；可要说别的，咱们俩都*太*太年轻了。看在*老天爷*份上，可*千万*别起念头给爸爸写信——反正往后再说也不迟——他和妈妈非*吓死*不可，他们至今还只当我是襁褓里的小娃娃呢。
> 开船之前没能回复你那封好意的信，你务必原谅我——说真的，我连*一分钟*也腾不出来。凡是*女人*都懂得，打点这样一趟出门是怎样一番鸡飞狗跳、人仰马翻：一趟趟跑*女裁缝*（*modiste*）铺子和女帽店——妈妈和我都发现，我们简直连一件能穿出门的行头也没有——还要跟朋友们道别，等等，等等，等等！不过我想，*区区一个男子*是懂不了的！
> 一路上愉快极了，只是起初我想家、想朋友，害了一阵*思乡病*（*mal-du-pays*）——望着水上的灯火，也许还格外想着某一位朋友，想着在利夫家最后一晚月光照在河上的模样。可我打定主意要「快活透顶」——船上一位汤普森先生（Mr. Thompson）成天把这话挂在嘴上——还打定主意一滴眼泪也不掉（这一条决心我不算*完全*守住！），一路上也结识了许多讨人喜欢的朋友。
> 嗯，起初*可怕*极了！船一颠一颠，颠得那么厉害！可过了两天就美了。我穿上了我那身新的蓝水手装，我们还看见了一头鲸鱼和一艘返航回国的船（望着*那艘船*，我忍不住轻轻叹了口气，不知你猜不猜得出为什么！），一朵朵小小的白浪花，像围着我们那条气派大船的船头嬉戏——有一天风实在大，水手们还得爬上桅索去收帆。（听我这口气，是不是很有几分航海气派？）我发现在海上过日子人人都饿得快，船上的*四*顿饭顿顿都吃到十分尽兴：八点半早饭，十二点午饭，四点正餐，八点宵夜。
> 船上自然少不了几场火热朝天的调情。大家都说，汤普森先生该改名叫「*情种*」先生才对，不过他满肚子玩笑，是全船的开心果。还有一位巴伦先生（Mr. Baron），一位懒洋洋的时髦青年，我在纽约的几场舞会上见过他，于是不消说，他觉得自己义不容辞，凡不睡着的当口就得活转过来对我大献殷勤——而那种当口*通常*寥寥。他母亲和妹妹随他同行，只是巴伦太太（Mrs. Baron）被*晕船*（*mal-du-mer*）折磨得露不了面，至于巴伦小姐嘛，我*可*不喜欢：她是个十足的调情好手，而且看样子也不大喜欢太太小姐们的圈子。我看船上的先生们比太太小姐们可爱多了；等航程结束的时候，大家已经好得像相识多年——因为我们那条船走得慢，正如汤普森先生说的：「一头老驴也走得比它快。」日子却魔法似的溜走了，虽然我们一个个闲得很——说真的，先生们像是把生意上的心思统统留在了岸上，太太小姐们撒起欢来更是*毫无保留*。有时晚上大家玩「路得与雅各」（“Ruth and Jacob”）的游戏，人人上阵，好不热闹；有时就干脆坐到船头去，望着浪花，试着望进未来。我那些同伴要是知道我的心思正飘到哪儿去，准会大吃一惊的，维克托！
> 如今我们到了伦敦，住进一处挺舒服、只是相当阴暗的寓所！想想看！我简直不敢相信。这地方看上去脏得很，我知道上了街我准会吓得要命，而且街上想必满都是狄更斯笔下那些可怕的人物。这会儿地板还在一上一下地晃，就跟「海华沙号」（Hiawatha）好船的甲板一个样——但愿它今晚之前消停下来才好，妈妈和我今晚要跟汤普森先生去看戏，看热纳维耶芙·沃德（Geneviéve Ward）主演的一出戏，剧名我*想*是《勿忘我》（“Forget-me-not”）。巴伦先生也来过了，戴着一双淡紫色羊皮手套，抱着一大束花（*这就*献起花来了！）请我们明晚去听歌剧，听帕蒂（Patti）唱歌。人们多热心呀，这不是有趣极了么？
> 这是我这辈子写过的最长的一封信。你寄给我的那枝铃兰，我夹进了祈祷书里，你猜它*偏偏*落在哪儿了——正落在婚礼仪文上，落在新娘说「我愿意」的那一句上！！！
> 爱你的
 

 露西。

===Original===
> I love getting your letters, but *really* you mustn’t write so often﻿—you really, really mustn’t. Mother said I must tell you.
> Victor, how can you think that I could forget you? You hurt me so when you say things like that. I think of you all the time, and I only don’t tell you *what* I think because I don’t want to make you vain.
> I like London now, it is full of interesting sights, but still I think it is pretty dirty and *old* looking. The gold on the Albert Memorial *certainly* needs rubbing up. We (Mother, Carter Thompson, Percy Baron, and Ethel Baron, whom I like much better now) went to Saint Paul’s, and got through our sightseeing in time to join in the choral service, with a *large* choir of men and boys. *I* think it would be better if they were given dusters and sent round to dust off the statues which are in every corner. We also attended choral service at Westminster Abbey, where the boys’ voices were simply angelic but the *intoning* of the minister almost put us to sleep﻿—*much* too High Church in my humble opinion. You will think we are very *pious*, but we have been to other places of interest too, Madame Tussaud’s Wax Works, where Carter simply *convulsed* us by taking the doorkeeper of the Chamber of Horrors for one of the figures, and the British Museum. We spent this afternoon at the latter place, but it will take *fully* another day to see *all* the wonders it contains.
> I don’t know what you mean, Victor, by saying you feel as if I had forgotten you and home. I would *die* if I thought I was going to spend my life over here. I *don’t* think things here are *nearly* as nice as at home, and the British public amuses me *very much*; for while the gentlemen are very handsome, the ladies are dowdy and dress about a year behind our fashions. Mother and I haven’t seen *any* bustles as big as ours, and as for the “aesthetes” they simply don’t wear any, and look perfectly *ridiculous*, going around in slinky sage green and brick red. The men aesthetes don’t cut their hair, and the women apparently never comb theirs, and they all sort of gasp at you. I was telling Carter Thompson about a tea in an artist’s studio Mother and I went to, and he said, “Oh, yes, they hug their knees and stick their chins out and yearn towards a sunflower or a blue china pot!” I nearly died laughing, as it really was a *perfect* description! Carter pretended to be astonished that we had anything to eat (we had heaps of things, and the most *heavenly* strawberries, and cream so thick you had to take it with a ladle). He said, “I thought they always just lunched on a lily.” He really is a perfect pickle. One afternoon when, needless to say, it *rained*, we went to an exhibition of Whistler’s paintings that he calls *nocturnes*; and Carter made us all laugh by saying “In my humble opinion *Daubs on Blotting Paper* would be a more appropriate name!” I must say they were all Greek to me, including a sort of insect he paints in the corner of each one.
> I am *longing* to ride in a hansom cab, but ladies don’t do it, alas! Promise not to be shocked to death and don’t breathe it to a soul, and I’ll tell you something *awful* Ethel and I did with the boys. Mother thought Mrs. Baron was going to matronize us, and Mrs. Baron thought Mother was going to play propriety, so we four went off for an afternoon of sightseeing, all alone! But that isn’t the worst, for we decided to come home in an omnibus just for a lark, but *all* the omnibuses going our way were full. We hesitated, but the boys’ saying we didn’t *dare* ride on top gave us courage; and we mounted amid the smiles of the bystanders, who evidently thought we were plucky little women to brave the criticisms of the people. Don’t tell *a soul*, for if Mother ever heard a word of it she’d lock me up on bread and water.
> You’ll get tired of reading these long epistles. Don’t forget me, Victor! I think of you every single second.
> Lucy.

===Chinese===
> 收到你的信我高兴得很，可是*说真的*，你不该写得这么勤——真的，真的不该。妈妈吩咐我一定得告诉你。
> 维克托，你怎么会以为我会忘了你？你说这样的话，真叫我伤心。我时时刻刻都在想你，我只是不告诉你*我想的是什么*，因为我不想把你宠得自高自大。
> 我如今喜欢上伦敦了，满城都是有趣的景致，不过我还是觉得它脏，样子也*老气*。阿尔伯特纪念塔（the Albert Memorial）上的镏金*确实*该擦擦亮了。我们（妈妈、卡特·汤普森、珀西·巴伦，还有如今我喜欢多了的埃塞尔·巴伦）去了圣保罗大教堂，观光赶巧赶上了唱诗礼拜，有一支*庞大的*男声男童唱诗班。*我*倒觉得，不如发给他们鸡毛掸子，打发他们把犄角旮旯里的雕像都掸一掸灰。我们还在威斯敏斯特教堂参加了一次唱诗礼拜，男童们的嗓音简直像天使，可那位牧师*吟诵*经文的调子差点儿把我们送入梦乡——依我愚见，实在是*太*高教会派了。你准以为我们*虔诚*得很，其实我们也去了别处名胜：杜莎夫人蜡像馆（Madame Tussaud’s）——卡特把「恐怖刑房」（Chamber of Horrors）的看门人错当成了蜡人，把我们都*笑翻了*；还有大英博物馆。今天下午我们就是在博物馆里过的，可要把馆里的奇珍*统统*看遍，*足足*还得再花上一整天。
> 维克托，你说你觉得我好像忘了你、忘了家，我不懂你这是什么意思。要是我以为自己得在这一边过一辈子，我*宁可死*了才好。我*不*觉得这边的东西*有*家里的一半好；英国的百姓可把我*逗坏*了：先生们个个英俊，太太们却穿得邋遢，衣裳比我们的时新款式落后了差不多一年。妈妈和我就没见过*一个*人的腰垫大得过我们的；至于那些「唯美派」，他们干脆一个腰垫也不束，穿着软塌塌的鼠尾草绿和砖红色招摇过市，*滑稽*透了。唯美派的男人不剪头发，女人们看样子压根儿不梳头，见了人还都一副喘不上气来的样子望着你。我跟卡特·汤普森说起妈妈和我去参加过的一场画家工作室茶会，他说：「哦，可不是，一个个抱着膝盖、戳着下巴，朝一朵向日葵或一只蓝瓷罐痴痴地向往！」我差点笑死，这形容*再贴切不过*了！卡特还假装吃惊，说我们居然有东西吃（其实点心堆得像小山，还有*天堂似*的草莓，奶油稠得非用长柄勺舀不可）。他说：「我还当这种人向来只拿一朵百合当午饭呢。」他真是个不折不扣的活宝。有天下午——不消说，那天下*雨*——我们去看了惠斯勒（Whistler）的画展，他把那些画称作*「夜曲」*（*nocturnes*）；卡特逗得我们全笑了，他说：「依我愚见，叫*《吸墨纸上的涂鸦》*兴许更名副其实！」我得承认那些画对我来说如同天书，连他在每一幅角上画的那只小虫子也一样参不透。
> 我*巴望*着坐一回双轮小马车，可惜淑女们不兴这么坐！答应我别吓个半死，也别向一个活人透露半个字，我就告诉你一件埃塞尔和我跟着男孩子们干下的*了不得的*事。妈妈以为巴伦太太会来照管我们，巴伦太太又以为妈妈会来把守礼数——结果我们四个自己跑出去观光了整整一个下午！这还不算最糟的：我们一时兴起，决定坐公共马车回家，偏偏*所有*往我们那个方向去的车都满了。我们正犹豫，男孩子们起哄说我们*不敢*坐车顶，这一激反倒给我们壮了胆；我们就在旁观者的微笑中登上了车顶，他们显然把我们当成了敢犯众人非议的小勇女子。*一个字*也别告诉*任何人*，妈妈要是听见一丝风声，准把我关起来，只给面包和白水吃。
> 这些长信你迟早要读腻的。别忘了我，维克托！我每一秒、每一秒都在想你。
> 露西。

===Original===
> **Victor dear**, I cried when I read your letter. I love to have you feel that way about me, but I’m not worthy of it.
> I did wish for you yesterday. We went to Hampton Court, which is very historical, but the flowers are lovely. I saw a brown butterfly on a blue Canterbury bell, they were just the colors of that old dress I was wearing the first day we met, when you pretended you thought some bows of ribbon on it were butterflies﻿—do you remember? But I know you don’t.
> You needn’t be jealous of Mr. Thompson, for I never was so disappointed in anyone in my *whole life*. He is *not* a gentleman. Yesterday at Hampton Court he made an excuse to get me away from the others, to feed the swans, *he said*, and then proposed, apparently taking it for granted that my answer would, *of course*, be “yes.” When I said, which was true, that I was completely taken by surprise, he said “Tell us another one!” and that I *had led him on*! I never want to see him again as long as I live, and I cried myself to sleep last night.
> Oh, Victor, I do miss you so! And I do *love you*!
> Your own
 

 Lucy.

===Chinese===
> **亲爱的维克托**，读你的信时我哭了。你对我存着这样的心思，我很欢喜，可我配不上。
> 昨天我倒真盼着你在。我们去了汉普顿宫（Hampton Court），那地方古得很、满是历史，花却可爱极了。我看见一只褐色蝴蝶落在一朵蓝色的风铃草上，那颜色，正跟我们初次见面那天我穿的那条旧裙子一模一样——那天你假装把裙上的缎带蝴蝶结认成了蝴蝶——你还记得吗？不过我知道你不记得了。
> 你用不着嫉妒汤普森先生，我*这辈子*从来没对谁这样失望过。他*算不上*一位绅士。昨天在汉普顿宫，他寻了个由头把我从众人身边引开——*说是*去喂天鹅——接着就求起婚来，那副样子，显然认定我*理所当然*会答应「好」。我说我全然没有料到——这可是真话——他却说「编个像样点的吧！」还说我*一直在吊他*！我这辈子再不想见他了，昨晚我是哭着哭着才睡着的。
> 哦，维克托，我多么想你呀！我是*真的爱你*！
> 属于你的
 

 露西。

===Original===
> I wrote you a dreadfully silly letter yesterday, which you mustn’t pay any attention to. I was tired and nervous, and got somewhat *hysterical*.
> We leave tomorrow for Windermere. I am looking forward very much to seeing the Lakes. We will be travelling about so much that I’m afraid I won’t be able to write very often, the days are so full and I am so tired when evening comes. But even if I haven’t time to write I will often be thinking of all my friends at home.
> Ever your true friend,
 

 Lucy.

===Chinese===
> 昨天我给你写了一封傻透傻透的信，你千万别放在心上。我又累又紧张，多少有些*歇斯底里*。
> 我们明天动身去温德米尔（Windermere）。我一心盼着看看湖区。接下来我们要四处奔波，恐怕不能常常写信了——白天排得满满当当，到了晚上人又累得很。可就算没工夫写信，我也会时时刻刻想着家里的各位朋友。
> 你永远忠实的朋友，
 

 露西。

===Original===
> Oh, Victor, what made me post such a *horrible* letter to you this morning? Can you ever forgive me and love me again? I didn’t mean *one word* of it! Victor, if you ever stop loving me I will die.
> Your heartbroken
 

 Lucy.

===Chinese===
> 哦，维克托，今天早晨我怎么会把那样一封*可恶的*信寄给你？你还能原谅我、重新爱我吗？那信里的话，我*一个字*也不是真心的！维克托，你若有一天不爱我了，我就活不成了。
> 你那颗伤碎了心的
 

 露西。

===Original===
> I’m so ashamed of not having written for such *ages*, but we have been on the go so hard. If I wrote a letter every time I *thought* of you, you would be swamped, and anyway I *cannot* write about the beautiful and quaint and wonderful things I see in a way that will convey any idea of their loveliness. Sir Walter Scott has described “Bonny Scotland” much better than I can, and then so many things over here are remarkable for nothing else than their oddness. But how often I wish you were here to see everything with me!
> We are seeing things under the very *best* auspices, as a *very* nice young man, the Honorable Ronald Marcy-Prince, who is travelling with his tutor, has practically attached himself to us, and is *most* kind about escorting the Mater and me on sightseeing expeditions when the Pater prefers a nap (which I must confess is *most* of the time.) The Honorable Ronald is the son of Lord Burketter, if you please, so we have the *greatest* attention wherever we go! I must say the importance the English attach to a *title* amuses me *intensely*!
> Scotland is *adorable* but so misty it is hard to keep your bangs looking like anything, and for the last two days we have been *enjoying* (?) a pouring rain storm. I am too sleepy to keep my eyes open any longer, but will write a *real letter* soon.
> Your affectionate friend,
 

 Lucy.
> P.S.﻿—I enclose a gorse flower﻿—it is *gloriously beautiful* growing, golden yellow and a fragrance all its own, but with lots of thorns. The Honorable Ronald (ahem!) taught me a saying the English have about it﻿—“When the gorse is out of bloom, then kissing’s out of fashion.” Can you guess why I am sending you a piece?

===Chinese===
> 隔了*这么久*没写信，我羞愧得很，可我们一路奔波得太厉害了。倘若我每*想*你一回就写一封信，你怕是要被信件淹没；再说，眼前这些又美、又古雅、又奇妙的事物，我*实在*写不出能传达其可爱之万一的文字。「美丽的苏格兰」（“Bonny Scotland”），沃尔特·司各特爵士（Sir Walter Scott）早就写得比我好得多；再说这边许许多多东西，除了古怪之外也别无可取。可我多么常常地盼着你也在，陪我一道看这一切啊！
> 我们观光的机缘*再好不过*：一位*非常*讨人喜欢的年轻人，尊贵的罗纳德·马西-普林斯（the Honorable Ronald Marcy-Prince），正带着他的私人导师旅行，如今几乎是寸步不离地跟定了我们；每逢老爸（the Pater）宁可打盹的当口（我得承认，这*多半*是常态），他总*十分*热心地护送老妈（the Mater）和我出去游览。尊贵的罗纳德，您猜怎么着，正是巴克特勋爵（Lord Burketter）的公子！因此我们走到哪儿都受到*顶顶*隆重的礼遇！不得不说，英国人这样看重*头衔*，实在把我*乐坏了*！
> 苏格兰*可爱极了*，只是雾气太重，刘海怎么也梳不出个样子；这两天，我们一直在*享受*（？）一场倾盆大雨。我困得眼睛再也睁不开了，回头很快给你写封*正经的信*。
> 爱你的朋友，
 

 露西。
> 又启——随信附上一朵荆豆花——这花长在枝头时*美得堂皇*，金黄金黄，香气自成一格，只是刺儿不少。尊贵的罗纳德（咳咳！）教了我一句英国人的俗话——「荆豆花不开，亲吻便不时兴。」你猜不猜得到，我为什么单单送你这一朵？

## XXI / XXI

===Original===
Victor, like Lucy’s ship, went up and down, as his angel’s voice called to him from across the sea that she was missing him so that she nearly *died*, or that the gentlemen of Ireland were the most *awful* flatterers, but somehow you couldn’t get angry. He was working in the real estate office of Uncle Willie’s friend, Mr. Vernon Johnson. Every morning he yawned down the lane through the morning mists and boarded the 7:17 to Wilmington, carrying a lawyer’s green bag that May had made for him, that held three soda biscuits with ham inside them, three with apple jelly, an orange or a pear, and a volume of Zola in French to read on the train, more to impress his fellow travellers than for his own enjoyment.
He sat at his desk and licked stamps, led prospective customers to small brick houses, and swept out the office when the janitor was drunk, through a pink and gold and forget-me-not blue haze﻿—a rainbow dream of Lucy. Oh, he would think, yawning, gazing through the window, he would be rich some day! Lucy, riding behind prancing, perfectly matched horses and a perfectly matched coachman and footman in livery, and simply covered with rubies and pearls, would drive down to the office every afternoon to take him home. And he saw her more clearly than he saw the guttersnipe sparrows, cellar doors, and ashcans his eyes were resting on. Some day! At present his salary was thirty dollars a month.
All his daily life was a blur, a confused murmur, through which came sweet and clear Lucy’s messages to the effect that Holland was very flat and full of windmills, that the cathedral at Cologne was too magnificent for description, that at the table d’hôtes of Holland and Germany *they helped you to ice-cream twice*, that the Rhine was too beautiful for description, that thousands of plum trees filled the orchards in Prussia, but there seemed to be more apples in Bavaria, that peasants were quaint, that Milan was fascinating, that the pension at Naples had been horrid but very *Italian*, that Rome was simply beyond words, that there were mountains in Switzerland and laces in Brussels, and that the men of Germany, Italy, Switzerland and Belgium stared dreadfully.

===Chinese===
维克托也像露西坐的那艘船一样，起起落落——他的天使隔着大海传来声音，一会儿说她想他想得几乎要*死*，一会儿说爱尔兰的先生们是天底下*最要命的*谄媚高手，可不知怎么的，你就是恼不起来。他在威利叔叔的朋友弗农·约翰逊先生（Mr. Vernon Johnson）的房地产事务所里做事。每天清晨，他打着呵欠穿过晨雾、沿小巷走去，搭上 7:17 开往威尔明顿的火车，手里拎着梅给他缝的律师绿提包，包里装着三块夹火腿的苏打饼干、三块夹苹果冻的，一只橙子或一只梨，还有一卷法文版的左拉（Zola），预备在火车上读——与其说为了自己受用，不如说为了叫同车的旅人刮目相看。

他坐在办公桌前舔邮票，领着有意购房的主顾去看一幢幢小红砖房，看门人喝醉的日子就由他来打扫办公室——这一切都透着一层粉红、金黄和勿忘我蓝的薄雾——一场关于露西的彩虹色的梦。哦，他会这样想，一面打着呵欠，一面望着窗外：总有一天他会发财的！到那时，露西每天下午会驱车来到事务所门口接他回家——拉车的骏马昂首腾跃、匹匹毛色般配，连制服齐整的车夫和跟班也般配得一丝不差，而她浑身缀满红宝石和珍珠。眼前那几只街溜子似的麻雀、地窖门和煤灰桶，他目光虽落在上面，却远不如把她看得真切。总有一天！眼下，他的月薪是三十美元。

他的整个日常生活成了一片模糊、一片乱糟糟的低语，其中唯有露西的音讯又甜又清亮，大意是：荷兰非常平坦，遍地风车；科隆的大教堂壮丽得没法形容；在荷兰和德国的客饭（table d’hôtes）桌上*人家会给你上两回冰淇淋*；莱茵河美得没法形容；普鲁士的果园里满是成千上万株李树，巴伐利亚的苹果倒似乎更多；乡下人古趣盎然；米兰迷人得很；那不勒斯的膳宿公寓糟透了，却*很有意大利味儿*；罗马简直是言语道不尽；瑞士有的是山，布鲁塞尔有的是花边；还有德国、意大利、瑞士和比利时的男人，盯起人来可吓死人。

===Original===
“We met a charming young American, named Mr. Duncan, in Amsterdam, full of fun but I’m afraid rather *fast*,” wrote Lucy, “He is always saying the funniest things. The other day he said ‘Did you ever notice that nearly everything here begins or ends with *dam*? I must say it sounds *rather* profane!’ I don’t suppose it was quite the thing to say before a *lady*, but he said it with such a solemn face I simply couldn’t help bursting out laughing!”
Down went Victor’s heart.

===Chinese===
「我们在阿姆斯特丹结识了一位迷人的美国青年，姓邓肯（Mr. Duncan），满肚子风趣，只是恐怕人有点*轻浮*，」露西写道，「他总有最逗乐的话。前几天他说：『你可曾留意，这儿差不多样样东西，开头或结尾都嵌着个「dam」？我得说，听上去*未免*有几分咒骂的味道！』我想这话在一位*小姐*面前说未必得体，可他板着那么一副严肃的面孔，我实在忍不住放声大笑！」

维克托的心往下一沉。

===Original===
“Venice is *absolutely* beyond the powers of my feeble pen. Mother, Father, and I went out in a gondola in the moonlight last night, and Father teased me for moping. The tears *were* very near, though I didn’t tell him *why*﻿—that I simply couldn’t *bear* being in *Venice*, in the *moonlight*, without you!”
Up flew his heart like a bird in the sky.

===Chinese===
「威尼斯*绝对*超出我这支拙笔的本事。昨晚妈妈、爸爸和我坐着贡多拉在月光里出游，爸爸还打趣我无精打采。眼泪*确曾*就在眼眶边上打转，只是我没告诉他*为什么*——在*威尼斯*，在*月光*里，身边却没有你，我简直*受不了*！」

他的心又像小鸟似的飞上了天。

===Original===
“Everywhere in Italy there are the most *perfect* flowers for sale on the streets, and Mr. Duncan, who just *happened* to turn up in Florence, simply *filled* our rooms with them, and sent me a bunch of *orange blossoms*, if you please, which I thought rather *saucy* to say the least!”
Down went his heart like a stone thrown into the sea.

===Chinese===
「意大利满大街卖的花，*完美*得无以复加。邓肯先生——他*偏偏*又在佛罗伦萨露面了——索性拿花把我们的房间*塞满*了，还给我送来一束*橙花*，您瞧瞧，依我看这至少可以说是*胆大放肆*！」

他的心又像一块石头似的，扑通沉进了海里。

===Original===
And longer and longer grew the silences between. But just as Victor would begin to emerge from unhappiness and hurt pride into freedom, she would call him back, lovingly and sweetly, to her side.

===Chinese===
而两人之间的音讯，隔得越来越久。可每每当维克托眼看就要从不快和受伤的自尊里脱身出来、走向自由，她又会甜甜地、亲亲热热地一声呼唤，把他唤回自己身边。

===Original===
“Victor! Here we are in *la Belle* France! *Paris*﻿—I can’t believe it. It is all my fancy painted it and *much*, *much* more, such a whirl, the *darlingest* dresses, though I can’t expect your highness to be interested in *them*, such gloves, such bonbons! Then the Opera, which is *divine*, I nearly cried my eyes out at ‘Faust’ the other night, and driving in the Bois, and all Mamma’s friends being *hospitality itself* to us. And yet I am homesick﻿—can you guess why?”

===Chinese===
「维克托！我们到*美丽的*法兰西（*la Belle* France）啦！*巴黎*——我简直不能相信。它全然是我梦想中描画出来的模样，还*远远*、*远远*不止于此：那样的一团令人眼花缭乱的热闹，*顶顶*可爱的衣裙——不过谅您殿下也未必对*它们*感兴趣——那样的手套，那样的夹心糖（bonbons）！然后是歌剧院，*圣洁*极了，前几天晚上看《浮士德》，我差点把眼睛都哭出来；还有坐着马车兜布洛涅林苑（the Bois），妈妈的朋友们对我们个个*好客到家*。然而我偏偏想家——你猜是为什么？」

===Original===
Then a long, long silence, no answer to all his letters, and just as he was getting bitter, sure she had forgotten him, came her photograph from Paul Delahaye in the Rue Lafitte, tinted to show faint pink cheeks and deep blue eyes. Darling little Lucy, so like herself, so unchanged, that he nearly died of love and happiness﻿—Lucy with the biggest bustle in the world, and the sweetest expression, her head a little to one side as if she were listening to the voices of the other angels.

===Chinese===
接着是长长久久的沉默，他写去的信全没有回音。就在他日渐心怀怨愤、认定她已经把他忘掉的时候，她的照片来了——拉菲特街（Rue Lafitte）保罗·德拉艾（Paul Delahaye）照相馆的手笔，上了淡彩，晕出浅浅的粉颊和深深的蓝眼。亲爱的小露西，活脱脱就是她本人，一点儿也没变，他简直爱得要死、幸福得要死——照片上的露西，系着全世界最大的一副腰垫，挂着天下最甜美的神情，小脑袋微微偏向一侧，仿佛正侧耳倾听别的天使们的声音。

===Original===
It was too precious to leave out for the girls to see every time they made his bed and filled his pitcher. He kissed it and hid it in a bureau-drawer under his silk muffler, a birthday present from Aunt Priscilla. There were other treasures there, all speaking to him of Lucy﻿—bits of dried vegetation, a slipper bow, a lace pocket-handkerchief, an embossed picture of clasped hands and forget-me-nots from a Christmas cracker, and all her letters.

===Chinese===
这照片太珍贵了，不能摆在外头，让女仆们每回来铺床、灌水壶时都看见。他吻了吻它，把它藏进五斗橱抽屉里，压在真丝围巾底下——那围巾是普丽西拉婶婶送的生日礼物。抽屉里还有旁的宝贝，件件都在向他诉说露西——几段干枯的花草、一只鞋上的蝴蝶结、一方花边手帕、一枚圣诞彩包爆竹里的压花画片（紧握的双手和勿忘我），还有她所有的信。

===Original===
And then, two years after she had sailed away, and just before she was to sail for home, another letter came.
There it lay on the hall table, white as a feather from an angel’s wing. He couldn’t even wait until he got it upstairs to his room. He tore it open and read:

===Chinese===
后来，在她扬帆远去两年之后、就在她即将启航回家之前，又来了一封信。

它就躺在门厅的桌上，白得像天使翅膀上掉下来的一根羽毛。他等不及把它拿到楼上自己屋里，当场就撕开来读：

===Original===
> I hate to write this letter, as I am so afraid it will hurt you, but I couldn’t bear to have you hear from anyone but me of my engagement. I am going to marry Count René de la Villeblanche, the son of one of Mamma’s great friends, and I am very happy.
> You and I were only children, weren’t we, Victor? I realize that now. But I shall always think of our friendship as one of the sweetest things in my life, and I will be wishing your happiness wherever you are and whatever you do. It is sad to say *adieu* to all the old times, and I hope you won’t quite forget.
> Your true friend,
 

 Lucy Hawthorn.

===Chinese===
> 我不愿写这封信，因为我很怕它会伤了你；可我订婚的事，我受不了让你从别人嘴里听说，只好由我亲口告诉你。我要嫁给勒内·德·拉·维尔布朗什伯爵（Count René de la Villeblanche）了，他是妈妈一位至交的儿子，我很幸福。
> 你我那会儿不过是两个孩子罢了，是不是，维克托？如今我才明白过来。但我会永远把我们这份情谊，当作我一生中最甜蜜的事情之一；无论你在哪里、做什么，我都愿祝你幸福。同旧日时光道一声*永别*（*adieu*）固然叫人难过，我希望你不至于全然忘却。
> 你忠实的朋友，
 

 露西·霍桑。

===Original===
“Is that you, Victor?” asked Maggie, coming up from the cellar, where she had been getting a jar of peach preserves for supper. “There’s a letter for you﻿—oh, you found it. Hurry up if you want to wash your hands, supper’s nearly ready.”
Victor started upstairs. “Oh, by the way,” he called over his shoulders, “Lucy’s going to marry a French count﻿—” and his voice suddenly broke into a loud sound, half squawk, half hiccup.

===Chinese===
「是你吗，维克托？」玛吉问。她刚从地窖上来，去取晚饭要吃的一罐蜜渍桃子。「有你一封信——哦，你已经拿到了。要洗手就快点儿，晚饭这就好了。」

维克托朝楼上走去。「哦，对了，」他扭过头来喊道，「露西要嫁人了，嫁给一位法国伯爵——」他的嗓音突然迸出一声响亮的怪声，一半像嘶叫，一半像打嗝。

===Original===
Maggie ran up the stairs after him. “Why, I thought she was engaged to you!”
“So did I﻿—my mistake, evidently.”
“But *Victor*﻿—”
“Oh, Maggie!” And he turned his head away, pressing it against the wall, but she could see that he was crying, and she answered his pain out of the depths of her own.
“Oh, my poor little brother, I know!”

===Chinese===
玛吉追着他跑上楼梯。「咦，我还当她是跟你订了婚的呢！」

「我也这么当来着——显然，是我弄错了。」

「可是*维克托*——」

「哦，玛吉！」他把头扭开，抵在墙上，可她看得出他在哭，她从自己伤痛的深处，回应着他的痛。

「哦，我可怜的小弟弟，我知道！」

===Original===
“Nobody knows! Nobody *can* know﻿—”
“Sit down on the stairs a minute﻿—no, the girls won’t come in till I call them, they’re picking currants in the truck patch. Here, take mine, it’s clean, for a wonder﻿—”
“I wish I was dead.”

===Chinese===
「没人懂！没人*能*懂——」

「在楼梯上坐一会儿吧——不碍事，我不喊，女仆们不会进来的，她们在菜园里摘醋栗呢。来，用我的，这条难得还干净——」

「我恨不得死了才好。」

===Original===
“The nasty little thing﻿—I’d like to wring her neck!” Maggie thought to herself, her arm tight around his shaking shoulders, and added aloud:
“Don’t, Victor, don’t, she isn’t worth it.”
He looked at her tragically through red eyes.
“You mustn’t say a word against Lucy, Maggie. It isn’t her fault﻿—I must have failed her some way﻿—”

===Chinese===
「这可恶的小东西——真想掐断她的脖子！」玛吉心里暗骂，一条胳膊紧紧搂住他瑟瑟发抖的肩膀，嘴上却接着说：

「别这样，维克托，别这样，她不值得你这样。」

他一双哭红的眼睛悲悲切切地望着她。

「你不许说露西一句坏话，玛吉。这不是她的错——一定是我有什么地方辜负了她——」

===Original===
“It’s her mother’s doing, I bet you anything. French count! French no-account more likely. Ambitious old schemer! I never could bear that woman!”
Oh, how could she help him? A passion of pity flooded her. How could she comfort her little brother?
“I only want her to be happy.”

===Chinese===
「这是她妈妈一手摆布的，我拿什么都肯跟你打赌。法国伯爵！还不如说法国的窝囊废。野心勃勃的老算计婆！那女人我从来就受不了！」

唉，她能怎么帮他呢？一阵怜惜的激情涌上她的心头。她能怎么安慰她的小弟弟呢？

「我只盼她幸福。」

===Original===
And in the dark night of his unhappiness one little star came out and shone faintly﻿—he couldn’t help knowing that he was “taking it” wonderfully.
“That’s the only thing,” Maggie said, her voice gritty with effort. “Don’t let love turn into bitterness. And pity Lucy because she’s hurt you﻿—you’ve only been hurt, it won’t be so hard for you.”

===Chinese===
而在他不幸的暗夜之中，升起了一颗小星星，幽幽地闪着微光——他没法不知道，自己把这桩事「挺」得漂亮极了。

「就是这个理，」玛吉说，嗓音因竭力克制而沙哑。「别让爱变成怨毒。要可怜露西，就因为她伤了你——你受的只是伤，往后对你不会那么难熬。」

===Original===
But Victor, still shaking with sobs, did not hear her, for he was looking at Lucy at the end of a vista of years, a tragic figure saying to him through her tears:
“Ah Victor! If only﻿—”

===Chinese===
可是维克托还在抽噎得浑身发抖，没有听见她的话，因为他正隔着岁月的长廊，望着廊子尽头的那位露西——一个悲戚的身影，噙着眼泪对他说：

「啊，维克托！要是当初——」

## XXII / XXII

===Original===
Ridiculous to feel positively lightheaded with high spirits when one is in the middle thirties﻿—Maggie couldn’t understand it. Yet that was how she did feel. She wanted to go bounding up into the sky, like a balloon, to sing, shout at the top of her lungs, laugh, not just a little, but loud, with her head back and her mouth wide open. What had gotten into her? She hadn’t felt like this for heaven knew how many years. It was the spring, perhaps. Just the first beginning of spring, the soft boundless sky, the flowing wakening air, the sudden, sweet warmth of the sun, the smell of everything, that made her want to laugh and cry at the same time, not gently or sentimentally, but as hard as she could. Spring is not gentle or sentimental, for all its pretty pink and white cloak of blossoming boughs. The wet, new green in the woods, intense in the sunlight, is as fierce as a sword-thrust, flowers in the grass are as awful as stars in the sky. Spring is terrible and divine, tearing the earth wide open, tearing the children of earth.
She really wanted to go to the wedding in Wilmington﻿—not because Victor was going to be an usher, but just because she was bursting for some fun. She hadn’t anything fit to wear, but never mind﻿—her black silk covered her, and nobody would be looking at her, anyway.
But after she put it on, it looked so poky and old-lady that she cut some geraniums as bright as wet, scarlet paint, and a velvet leaf, to put in her belt. She didn’t care if it was silly, and her cape would cover them up on the train. And whisking around, getting tea and bread and butter and cottage-cheese, for they didn’t need much supper with a wedding reception to look forward to, she sang a song she hadn’t thought of for ever so long.

===Chinese===
一个人都三十五六了，居然还会高兴得晕晕乎乎，这岂不荒唐——玛吉自己也不明白。可她实实在在就是这样觉得。她恨不得一下子蹿上天去，像只气球，想唱歌，想扯开嗓子喊，想笑——不是浅浅一笑，而是放声大笑，头往后仰，嘴张得老大。她这是怎么了？天知道有多少年没这样过了。兴许是春天的缘故。才是春头上一丁点儿：天空温柔而无边，空气流淌着、苏醒着，太阳忽然暖得那么甜，样样东西都冒出气味——叫她恨不得又笑又哭，不是轻轻地、多愁善感地，而是使足全身力气地又笑又哭。春天并不温柔，也不多愁善感，任它披着满枝繁花那身粉白娇美的斗篷。林间湿润的新绿，在阳光下浓烈夺目，凌厉如一记剑刺；草丛里的花，庄严可畏如天上的星。春天可怕而又神圣，把大地豁然撕开，也撕扯着尘世的儿女。

她实在想去威尔明顿参加那场婚礼——倒不是因为维克托要充当引宾员，只因她浑身憋着一股劲儿，想乐一乐。她没有一件拿得出手的衣裳，可那也不要紧——黑绸裙好歹遮得住身，再说，反正也没人会看她。

可是穿上以后，那身黑绸瞧着土气，一副老太太相，于是她剪了几枝天竺葵——红得像湿润的朱红颜料——又摘了一片丝绒似的叶子，预备别在腰带上。傻不傻，她才不管呢，反正上了火车有斗篷罩着。她手脚麻利地转来转去张罗晚饭——茶、黄油面包、农家奶酪，晚上有婚宴可盼，晚饭不必多——嘴里唱起一支好久好久都没想起过的歌。

===Original===
> “ ‘I feel, I feel, I feel,
 

 I feel like a morning star!
 

 I feel, I feel, I feel,
 

 I feel like a morning star!
 

 Shoo fly! Don’t bother me!
 

 Shoo fly! Don’t bother me﻿—’ ”

===Chinese===
> 「『我觉得，我觉得，我觉得，
 

 我觉得像一颗晨星！
 

 我觉得，我觉得，我觉得，
 

 我觉得像一颗晨星！
 

 嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我！
 

 嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我——』」

===Original===
Squeak, squeak, squeak, went the pump-handle, out gushed the water. Clash! went the kettle cover. Maggie took a few dance steps on the kitchen floor.

> “ ‘Shoo fly! Don’t bother me!’

“Ma-ay! Plenty of extra hot water, if you want any! Get-*tout*, kitty, or I’ll step on you!

> “ ‘Shoo fly! Don’t bother me!’
 

 For I belong to Company G!’ ”

===Chinese===
吱扭，吱扭，吱扭，水泵的把手响个不停，水哗哗地涌出来。哐啷！壶盖跳了一下。玛吉在厨房地板上踏了几个舞步。

> 「『嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我！』」

「梅——！热水多得很，要用就来舀！*去*——边儿去喽，猫咪，不然我可要踩着你啦！

> 「『嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我！
 

 因为我属于G连！』」

===Original===
“Maggie! I’ve ripped under my arm!” That was Lily’s wail.
“Wait a minute﻿—I’ll come and sew you up.

> “ ‘Shoo fly! Don’t bother me!
 

 Shoo fly﻿—’ ”

===Chinese===
「玛吉！我胳肢窝底下绽线了！」那是莉莉的哭腔。
「等一下——我就来给你缝上。

> 「『嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我！
 

 嘘，苍蝇——』」

===Original===
May was a picture in dark red, so much handwork that it made your eyes ache to think of it, and with Mamma’s garnet necklace. In the soft afternoon light you couldn’t see the fine lines that had come around her mouth and eyes. Lily was in snuff color, with dark blue velvet bows. It was her best dress. She hadn’t worn it for months, and it had become ever so much too tight. She couldn’t take a long breath, she couldn’t lift her arms. Her nose was pink, and tears stood in her eyes.
“Goodness, Lily! This silk gives with every stitch I take. Stop breathing﻿—turn around to the light﻿—turn round. Oh, Lily! I do believe you’ve got a big spot on the front﻿—”
“I just can’t go,” Lily quavered.

===Chinese===
梅穿一身深红，美得像幅画——那上头手工多得想一想都叫人眼睛发酸——又戴着妈妈的石榴石项链。午后柔和的光线里，你看不出她嘴角和眼角新添的细纹。莉莉穿一身鼻烟色，缀着深蓝丝绒蝴蝶结。这是她最好的一条裙子，好几个月没上身，如今紧得不得了：她深呼吸不成，胳膊抬不起来，鼻子通红，眼泪含在眼眶里直打转。

「天哪，莉莉！这绸子不禁针，我缝一针它就让一针。别喘气——转到亮处来——转过来。哦，莉莉！我看你前襟准是蹭上一大块渍子了——」

「我干脆不去了，」莉莉声音发颤地说。

===Original===
“Let me think﻿—” And she tried to frown and think about poor fat Lily, but she began to smile, to hum:

> “ ‘I feel, I feel, I feel﻿—’

Look﻿—you can take Mamma’s lace shawl, it’ll hide everything if you’re just a little bit careful.”

===Chinese===
「让我想想——」她努力板起脸，想替可怜的胖莉莉操操心，可嘴角不由自主漾开笑来，嘴里也哼起了：

> 「『我觉得，我觉得，我觉得——』

 瞧——你可以披妈妈的蕾丝披肩，只要稍许当心一点儿，什么都能遮住。」

===Original===
They had put on their best bustles that had been Christmas presents from Aunt Priscilla. For every day May wore a homemade horsehair pad, and Maggie and Lily rolls of soft paper. These best ones were so large and elaborate that they made the sisters feel fashionable but apprehensive. Lily’s was forever getting to one side, and when it came to sitting down gracefully, they all had their bad moments.
They walked down the lane to the station, holding their skirts well up out of the spring mud. Their party slippers, and Cousin Jennie Blodgett’s Christmas present gloves still folded in yellow tissue paper, were in bags that swung from their arms, and white zephyr nubias covered their careful coiffures.

===Chinese===
她们戴上了各自最好的腰垫——都是普丽西拉婶婶送的圣诞礼物。平日里，梅垫自家做的马鬃垫，玛吉和莉莉垫软纸卷。这几副最好的腰垫又大又讲究，姐妹们戴着自觉时髦，心里又发怵。莉莉那副动不动就歪向一边，至于要坐得优雅嘛，三个人谁都有过出丑的时刻。

她们沿着小巷往车站去，一只手把裙裾高高提着，避开春日的泥泞。赴宴的便鞋，还有珍妮·布洛杰特表姐（Cousin Jennie Blodgett）送的圣诞手套——仍叠在黄色薄纸里没打开——都装在布袋里，挎在臂弯上晃悠；一丝不苟梳好的发髻上，罩着白细绒头巾。

===Original===
“Mercy, this mud is awful﻿—go slowly, girls, we have heaps of time.”
“Lily, did you﻿—look out, don’t catch your cloud on those twigs. Wait a minute﻿—hold still, I’ll get it off﻿—there! Did you remember to put kitty out?”
“Just imagine how the bride must be feeling by now﻿—look, that little patch of grass is ever so green!”

===Chinese===
「哎哟，这泥真够呛——慢着点儿走，姑娘们，咱们时间多着呢。」

「莉莉，你有没有——当心，别让云纱巾挂上那些枝条。等等——别动，我给你摘下来——好啦！你记得把猫放出去没有？」

「想想新娘子这会儿心里是什么滋味——瞧，那一小片草地绿得多嫩！」

===Original===
> “ ‘Shoo fly! Don’t bother me!
 

 Shoo fly! Don’t bother me!’ ”

===Chinese===
> 「『嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我！
 

 嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我！』」

===Original===
“What’s got into you, Maggie? Hold your skirt up higher in the back.”
“Oh, girls, *smell that spring smell*!”
And, as they stepped out of the Wilmington station, the big warm drops began to fall, making dark stars on the pavement. Great, warm, wet splashes of spring rain, falling on her face. Maggie was happy enough to cry.
“We’ll take a cab.”
“Have you enough with you?”
“Yes, it’s all right. We can’t walk in the rain in our silks, and with our hair and everything.”
They changed into their slippers and pulled on their white gloves, rumbling up Market Street.
“Look﻿—it’s raining hard now.”
“May, how much do you think I ought to fee the driver?”
“Girls! *Look* at the line of carriages, if you please! Did you ever?”
“Ten cents is *plenty*.”
“I know, but it’s so wet, and he looked sort of shabby.”
“See, they have a red carpet out﻿—I’m glad we changed into our slippers, they won’t get a bit wet.”
Maggie shoved some money into the driver’s hand and hurried after the others. May need never know how much it was, and anyway it was her own egg-money. He looked so forlorn, and his nose was so red, with a little drop trembling from it. She wanted everyone to be happy tonight.

===Chinese===
「你这是中了什么邪，玛吉？后头裙子再提得高些。」

「哦，姑娘们，*闻闻这春天的气息*！」

她们刚跨出威尔明顿车站，豆大的暖雨点便落下来，在人行道上砸出一颗颗深色的星。大片大片的春雨，又暖又湿，扑在她脸上。玛吉幸福得直想哭。

「咱们叫辆马车吧。」

「你带的钱够吗？」

「够，不碍事。咱们总不能穿着绸裙子冒雨走，何况还有这一头头发、这一身行头。」

马车辘辘驶上市场街（Market Street），她们换上便鞋，戴好白手套。

「瞧——雨下大了。」

「梅，你看我该赏车夫多少钱？」

「姑娘们！劳驾*瞧瞧*这排马车长龙！开过眼没有？」

「一毛钱*尽够*了。」

「我知道，可雨这么大，他瞧着又那么寒碜。」

「看，他们铺了红地毯——幸亏咱们换了便鞋，一点儿也淋不着。」

玛吉往车夫手里塞了把零钱，便匆匆追赶姐妹们去了。究竟给了多少，梅永远不必知道，反正花的是她自个儿卖鸡蛋攒的私房钱。车夫瞧着那么凄惶，鼻子冻得通红，鼻尖上还颤巍巍悬着一滴雨珠。她但愿今晚人人都快活。

===Original===
And then the church, and Victor. Lily pretended it was his wedding, and the idea melted her into tears. Where was her handkerchief? She must have brought one﻿—
“Maggie﻿—”
“Yessum.”
“I can’t find my handkerchief﻿—”
People smiled at each other with knowing little nods. They understood all about it, whatever it was. The organ warbled high and tremulous. Silk rustled past as the ushers towed lady after lady up the aisle.
“What, May?”
“The lilies are too sweet﻿—they make me feel faint.”
“Victor looks nice, doesn’t he? His coat’s just a teeny bit too big, but don’t ever tell him so.”
“There go the Hollys﻿—I should think she’d be just about dead with that sealskin sacque on a night like this, but let’s be stylish or die. I guess they must have driven in, they weren’t on the train.”
“They’re late﻿—the bride is, I mean. I wonder if anything could have happened.”
The organ rumbled so that Maggie couldn’t hear the rain, but she could feel it falling on her heart, life-giving and warm.
“Here they come!”
The organ agreed. Ta *dar*dy *da*! Ta *dar*dy *da*! The river of white silk and lace, pink sashes, and black coats, the river of life, flowed up the aisle.

===Chinese===
接着便是教堂，和教堂里的维克托。莉莉只当这是他自己的婚礼，这个念头把她化成了一汪泪水。她的手绢呢？总该带了一块的——

「玛吉——」

「是——太太。」

人们相视而笑，会意地点着小小的头。不管这是什么情由，他们全懂。管风琴高高地、颤颤地啭鸣。绸衣窸窣掠过，引宾员们把一位位女士沿通道引上前去。

「什么，梅？」

「百合香得太过了——熏得我发晕。」

「维克托今天真俊，是不是？他上衣就大了那么一丁点儿，可千万别跟他说。」

「霍利一家过去了——这样的夜晚她还裹着海豹皮短外套，不闷死才怪；不过嘛，不时髦，毋宁死。我猜他们准是自家赶车进城的，火车上没瞧见他们。」

「他们迟到了——我是说新娘子。别是路上出了什么事吧。」

管风琴轰隆隆地响，玛吉听不见雨声了，可她感觉得到雨落在她心上，温暖而滋养着生命。

「他们来啦！」

管风琴深表赞同。嗒*哒*底*呀*！嗒*哒*底*呀*！白绸与花边、粉红腰带与黑礼服汇成的河流，生命的长河，沿着通道涌了上来。

===Original===
The Hollys gave them a lift to the reception; and, as Mrs. Holly wanted to stop at her sister’s on the way, just for half a second, they were half an hour late. And then in the room where they took off their wraps Maggie saw a line of white petticoat showing under Lily’s skirt, and had to stop for that.
“Walk off, Lily﻿—nobody’s going to notice﻿—walk off a little way﻿—here, back up.”
“*Ouch*, Maggie!”
“Oh, did I run it into you? You’re all right now. Come on, girls!”
For two pins she’d slide down the banisters! Clatter, scream, a little thread of “Il Bacio” as they pushed slowly past the orchestra under the stairs. May talked to her sisters vivaciously, as if she had just been introduced to them, but her eyes wandered anxiously.

===Chinese===
霍利一家捎她们去婚宴；由于霍利太太要顺路在妹妹家停一停——就停那么半秒钟——她们迟到了半个钟头。随后，在解外套的那间屋子里，玛吉看见莉莉裙子底下露出一道白衬裙边，又不得不停下来收拾。

「往前走，莉莉——没人会留意的——走远一点儿——来，往后退。」

「*哎哟*，玛吉！」

「哦，扎着你啦？这就好了。走吧，姑娘们！」

只消有半分借口，她都想顺着楼梯扶手哧溜滑下去！一路哐啷作响、尖声欢叫，从楼梯底下的乐队旁慢慢挤过去时，还飘来一缕《吻》（Il Bacio）的曲调。梅跟两个妹妹谈笑风生，仿佛才刚经人引见认识她们，一双眼睛却焦急地四处张望。

===Original===
“What a lovely wedding!”
“Doesn’t the bride look sweet?”
What a lovely wedding! Doesn’t the bride look sweet? What a lovely wedding! Doesn’t the bride look sweet?
May had a man now, and was shrieking at him through the racket. Maggie and Lily were carried on by the tide.

===Chinese===
「多美的婚礼啊！」

「新娘子多甜啊！」

多美的婚礼啊！新娘子多甜啊！多美的婚礼啊！新娘子多甜啊！

梅如今有了个男人相陪，正隔着那片喧闹冲他尖声喊话。玛吉和莉莉叫人潮裹挟着往前涌。

===Original===
“See, Lily, how nice our present looks.”
They hadn’t been able to afford anything new. There were the pair of high-shouldered dark blue vases with the gold polka-dots and white stomachers painted with seaweed and shells, that had stood on the dining-room mantelpiece practically forever. How surprised they must be at their new surroundings, a blue velvet case of pearl-handled fish knives on one side, pink cake-plates with gold stippling on the other, and an onyx clock with a bronze Minerva behind them. It seemed almost disloyal to go away and leave them there﻿—such old friends.
Lily found a nice little corner where she was hidden away and didn’t have to bother about her bustle or her petticoat or keeping her shawl just so. When the colored waiter thrust the plate into her hands, she tried to explain that she had already had one supper, but he hurried away without paying any attention. So she ate the broiled oysters and chicken salad lingeringly, shedding a sentimental tear now and then when the violin, close by her ear, grew extra piercing.

===Chinese===
「瞧，莉莉，咱们送的礼多体面。」

她们买不起新东西。摆出来的正是那对高肩深蓝花瓶——金色波点，白色的胸饰板上画着海藻和贝壳——在饭厅壁炉台上摆了不知多少年了。面对新环境，它们该多么吃惊：一边是装珠柄鱼刀的蓝丝绒匣子，另一边是描金点彩的粉色蛋糕盘，背后还立着一座配青铜密涅瓦像的缟玛瑙座钟。就这样撇下它们走掉，简直近乎负心——这么老的老朋友了。

莉莉寻着了一个舒服的小角落，整个人躲进去，腰垫不用管了，衬裙不用管了，披肩正不正也不用管了。黑人侍者把盘子往她手里一塞，她还想解释自己已经用过一份晚饭，那人却头也不回地匆匆走开了。于是她慢条斯理地吃着烤牡蛎和鸡肉沙拉，贴在耳边的小提琴声格外尖利时，还不时洒下几滴伤感的泪。

===Original===
Maggie was just going to dive in after her when Mrs. Craig backed her against a palm.
“Oh, Miss Campion! Well! I haven’t seen you, since dear knows when﻿—how are you? Wasn’t it a pretty wedding? And dear little Annie made such a sweet bride, didn’t she? Have you had your supper? I thought the mayonnaise was sort of *poor*, didn’t you? But then I’m fussy about mayonnaise, I guess, at least so they always tell me﻿—and then there’s always so much celery in caterer’s chicken salad﻿—more of everything than chicken. I said to Mr. Craig, I guess the chicken this salad was made of had a mother that mooed and had horns and four legs﻿—look! That dress with the orange trimmings﻿—someone ought to tell her how she looks, really it would be kinder. Fat people ought to be careful what they wear.”
So they ought, thought Maggie, looking at Mrs. Craig’s round little body almost bursting out of its rose-colored silk. Just like a watermelon, and those jet buttons were the watermelon seeds.

===Chinese===
玛吉正要跟着莉莉往里钻，克雷格太太（Mrs. Craig）却把她顶到了一株棕榈跟前。

「哟，坎皮恩小姐！哎呀！天知道多久没见着您了——您好吗？婚礼够美的吧？亲爱的小安妮做新娘子多甜呀，是不是？晚饭用过了吗？我倒觉得那蛋黄酱有点*差劲*，您说呢？不过我这个人就是对蛋黄酱挑剔，他们老是这么说我——再说包席的鸡肉沙拉里，芹菜总是搁得太多——样样都比鸡肉多。我跟克雷格先生（Mr. Craig）说来着：我猜这沙拉用的那只鸡，它娘准是会哞哞叫、长犄角、四条腿的——瞧！那件镶橘色滚边的衣裳——真该有人告诉她瞧瞧自己是什么模样，说实在的，那才算积德。胖人穿衣裳就该当心着点儿。」

可不是嘛，玛吉心想，一面打量克雷格太太那圆滚滚的小身段，几乎要撑破那身玫瑰红绸衣。活脱脱一只西瓜，那些墨玉纽扣就是西瓜籽。

===Original===
> “ ‘I feel, I feel, I feel,
 

 I feel like a morning star﻿—’ ”

===Chinese===
> 「『我觉得，我觉得，我觉得，
 

 我觉得像一颗晨星——』」

===Original===
Oh, she must get that out of her head.

===Chinese===
哦，她非得把这歌从脑子里赶出去不可。

===Original===
“Frank! Coo-hoo! Frank! You’ve met my husband, haven’t you, Miss Campion? Frank, I could eat another plate of ice-cream if I was sufficiently urged, couldn’t you, Miss Campion? *Couldn’t* you? *Frank!* Miss Campion says she couldn’t. Just one, dear. Just one. *Frank!* Unless you want one yourself! Have you seen the presents? Well, to tell you the *honest* truth I was surprised they weren’t handsomer﻿—oh, stop that waiter﻿—waiter! Try this punch, I think there’s champagne in it﻿—”
Maggie moved her head away from the palm sticking into the back of her neck. Mercy, how the woman buzzed!

===Chinese===
「弗兰克（Frank）！哟——呼！弗兰克！您见过我丈夫的，对不对，坎皮恩小姐？弗兰克，要是有人肯使劲儿劝，我还能再吃一盘冰淇淋，您呢，坎皮恩小姐？*您*说呢？*弗兰克！*坎皮恩小姐说她不能。就一盘，亲爱的。就一盘。*弗兰克！*除非你自己也想要一盘！礼品你们看了没有？唉，跟您说句*老实话*，那些礼品不够气派，我真吃了一惊——哦，拦住那个侍者——侍者！尝尝这潘趣酒，我看里头搀了香槟——」

玛吉把头挪开，躲开那片戳着她后脖颈的棕榈叶。天哪，这女人可真聒噪！

===Original===
> “ ‘Shoo fly! Don’t bother me!’ ”

===Chinese===
> 「『嘘，苍蝇！别来烦我！』」

===Original===
“I was looking at the presents with Edward Post and his wife, and he was saying that the worst of getting married﻿—”
The glass of punch leapt on Maggie’s plate.
“Edward﻿—?”
“Edward Post. Didn’t you know him when he lived with the Allens, years ago, before he went to South America? You must have, they lived so near you. Why, yes, seems to me I’ve heard he was quite a beau of yours, haven’t I? Didn’t you see him and his wife this evening?”
Maggie set down her chattering glass and plate on the corner of the mantelpiece. She was shivering all over. Mrs. Craig’s face floated towards her, burst into splinters of brightness and blackness. She heard her own voice say, high and unfamiliar, an affected society voice:
“No, I didn’t see him. In fact, I hadn’t heard he was married.”
And she laughed nervously, politely, leaning against the wall to keep from falling.
“Oh, yes, indeed, very much so, he has been for two years. In fact he married a distant connection of Mr. Craig’s﻿—*too* bad you didn’t meet her, but they left very early. Of course, they don’t live here, but he comes on sometimes on business, I understand﻿—not that Mr. Craig and myself see much of them; just between you and me I think success has turned his head a little. She had a *very* pretty dress on, a pink corded silk, not exactly *pink*, more *peach*﻿—look! Will you *look* at Mr. Craig! He’s lost me completely﻿—look at that *hopeless* expression. See, he doesn’t see us at all! Frank! Oh, Frank, honey﻿—I’ll just have to chase after him﻿—”

===Chinese===
「方才我正跟爱德华·波斯特（Edward Post）和他太太一块儿看礼品来着，他就说，结婚最糟的一条就是——」

那杯潘趣酒在玛吉的盘子上跳了一下。

「爱德华——？」

「爱德华·波斯特。多年前他跟艾伦家（the Allens）住在一块儿、还没去南美那阵子，你不认识他吗？你一定认识，他们住得离你们那么近。哎呀对了，我好像听说过，他从前是你蛮热的一位情郎，是不是？今晚你没瞧见他们夫妇吗？」

玛吉把叮叮作响的杯盘搁到壁炉台角上。她浑身直打颤。克雷格太太的脸朝她浮过来，迸裂成一片明与暗的碎屑。她听见自己的声音开了口，又高又陌生，一副社交场上拿捏出来的腔调：

「没有，我没看见他。说实话，我都不知道他结了婚。」

她神经质地、客客气气地笑着，倚住墙壁，免得跌倒。

「哦，是啊，可不，结了都两年了。其实他娶的是克雷格先生的一位远亲——*真*可惜你没见着她，他们走得很早。当然喽，他们不住在这儿，不过我听说他有时因公过来——倒不是说我跟克雷格先生和他们有多少来往；就你知我知吧，我看是功成名就叫他有点忘乎所以。她今晚穿了件*顶*漂亮的衣裳，粉色横棱绸，也不全是*粉*，更近乎*蜜桃色*——瞧！你*瞧瞧*克雷格先生！他把我彻底丢了——瞧瞧那副*无可救药*的表情。看，他压根儿没瞧见我们！弗兰克！哦，弗兰克，亲爱的——我只得追他去了——」

===Original===
Maggie went upstairs to the bedroom, empty except for heaps of cloaks, drifts of nubias, foothills of storm-boots, and huddled in a dark corner. Her geraniums fell from her belt and lay on the floor beside her, as red as a pool of blood. She felt as if she were bleeding to death. She had never really said goodbye in her heart to Edward. But now she knew that the footsteps she had listened to for so long would never reach her.

===Chinese===
玛吉上楼进了卧室——屋里空荡荡的，只剩一堆堆外套、雪堆似的绒头巾、小丘般的套靴——她蜷缩在一个黑暗的角落里。腰间的天竺葵落下来，躺在她身旁的地板上，红得像一汪血。她觉得自己仿佛在流血，快要流尽而死。在心里，她从来没有真正同爱德华道过别。可现在她知道了：她听了这么多年的那串脚步声，永远不会走到她跟前来了。

---

===Original===
> “ ‘Goodnight, ladies! I’m going to leave you now!’ ”

===Chinese===
> 「『晚安，女士们！我这就告辞啦！』」

---

===Original===
“I don’t feel a bit sleepy. Didn’t Victor look wonderful? I don’t think it’s prejudice, I think he really *was* far and away the best looking usher, don’t you? Just imagine the way we’ll be feeling when he’s the bridegroom!”
“Is he coming home or spending the night in town? Well, I’ll leave out some sponge-cake and milk anyway. Goodnight.”
“Goodnight.”
“Lily, did you see to kitty?”
“Someone stood all over my slippers﻿—look, one bow’s lost!”
“Goodnight, I guess I am sleepy after all. Oh, I brought home some wedding-cake to dream on﻿—want some, May? Maggie! Want some wedding-cake to dream on?”

===Chinese===
「我一点儿也不困。维克托今天是不是神气极了？我想这不是偏心，我想他*确实*是引宾员里最英俊的一个，把旁人远远甩在后头，你说呢？想想等到他做新郎那天，咱们心里会是什么滋味！」

「他是回家来，还是在城里过夜？唉，反正我给他留点儿海绵蛋糕和牛奶就是了。晚安。」

「晚安。」

「莉莉，猫照应好了吗？」

「不知谁在我便鞋上踩了个遍——瞧，一只蝴蝶结都踩丢了！」

「晚安——我到底还是困了。哦，我带了几块婚礼蛋糕回来，好搁在枕头底下做梦——要吗，梅？玛吉！要几块婚礼蛋糕搁着做梦吗？」

## XXIII / XXIII

===Original===
While we still feel that we are just entering the sea we look back, and how far we have come! How far away are the seashells and sandcastles of childhood. We can see them, but we can’t go back to them. No matter how tired or frightened we are, we have to swim on out to sea.
Forty-one years old! Maggie couldn’t believe it. She didn’t feel forty-one a bit! Of course, she was often tired now﻿—she, who had never been tired. But she got up so early and went so hard all day. And then sometimes she didn’t sleep very well, but that was when she let herself think of Edward.
Forty-one! Mamma had only been forty-two when she died﻿—impossible to be almost as old as Mamma, almost disrespectful. And she couldn’t feel grownup inside her﻿—or did older people have these young feelings﻿—were they shy, not quite sure of themselves, glowing with love at a kindness, delighted with something pretty to wear or something good to eat, and sometimes wanting more than anything to stick out their tongues and make faces﻿—and did they just hide them under dark clothes and quiet ways?

===Chinese===
我们自觉才刚刚游进大海，一回头，竟已游出这么远！童年的贝壳与沙堡，都已隔着千山万水。我们望得见它们，却再也回不去了。无论多疲惫、多惶恐，都只能继续朝深海游去。

四十一岁了！玛吉简直不敢相信。她一点儿也不觉得自己有四十一岁！当然，她如今常觉疲惫——她这个从不知道累的人。可她起得那么早，一整天又忙个不停。有时夜里也睡不安稳，那是她任由自己去想爱德华的时候。

四十一！妈妈去世时也不过四十二——眼看就要活得跟妈妈一样老了，简直不可思议，简直大不敬。她心里怎么也长不出大人的感觉——莫非上了年纪的人心里也揣着这些年轻的心绪——也会害羞，也会拿不准自己，受人一点好处就暖流满怀，为一件漂亮衣裳或一样好吃的东西欢喜不尽，有时还恨不得吐吐舌头做做鬼脸——只不过他们把这些都藏在深色衣裳和安静做派底下罢了？

===Original===
The Campions were so poor that they were all trying to earn a little extra money. Twice a week Maggie drove in to The Woman’s Exchange with her cakes and May’s lampshades and little crêpe paper baskets to hold candies or ice-cream. Such pretty baskets, pale violet with purple paper violets tied with a bow of baby ribbon to the handle, pale green with buttercups, and pink with something charming, though none of them quite knew what. Or sometimes the baskets were like big cabbage roses. When there was a special order Maggie would help. Lily longed to help too, but her clumsy fingers tore the thin tissue paper and dropped glue on May’s exquisite petals, and her violets weren’t violets, but only crumpled balls, good for nothing but for the kitten to pat across the room and pounce on. So she read aloud to them while they worked, *Ships That Pass in the Night* and *Sweet Bells Out of Tune*, nearly yawning her head off, and driving them wild, by pausing every now and then to read ahead to herself a little, just to see what was going to happen.

===Chinese===
坎皮恩家穷了，人人都在想法子多挣几个钱。玛吉每星期两回赶车进城，上妇女交换所（The Woman's Exchange）去，带上她的蛋糕、梅的灯罩，还有装糖果或冰淇淋的绉纸小篮。多俊的篮子啊：淡紫的缀着纸做的深紫罗兰，柄上以细缎带系一个蝴蝶结；淡绿的配毛茛花；粉红的配着一种说不清却怪迷人的什么花。有时篮子又做成大朵卷心菜玫瑰的模样。有特别订货时，玛吉就来搭把手。莉莉也一心想帮忙，可她那笨手指不是撕破薄纸，就是把胶水滴到梅精巧的花瓣上；她做的紫罗兰不成其为紫罗兰，只是一团团皱纸球，只配给小猫当玩具，让它一路拍到屋子那头再扑上去。于是趁她们干活，她便给她们朗读，念《夜里交臂而过的船》（Ships That Pass in the Night）和《走调的甜钟》（Sweet Bells Out of Tune），呵欠打得下巴都要掉了；还时不时停下来，自己偷偷往下读几句，先瞧瞧后文要发生什么，把两个姐姐都快逼疯了。

===Original===
She was trying to earn something, too, by giving music lessons. She wasn’t very accurate, but her four pupils were all beginners, and she could play over their pieces, “Joy and Frolic Galop,” “The Little Penitent,” “The Brooklet,” or “Dolly’s Funeral,” with a great deal of ripple and expression. She had Sissie and Mary Holly, Stewy Grant’s little boy, and Miss Taylor, the postmaster’s daughter; and most of the lesson times were taken up in cozy chats, telling her troubles, offering freshly baked cookies and having one or two herself, or taking her pupils into the garden to see Maggie’s Oriental poppies.

===Chinese===
她也在教音乐挣钱。她弹得算不上顶准，好在四个学生全是初学，那些曲子她都能弹给他们听：《欢乐嬉戏加洛普》（"Joy and Frolic Galop"）、《小小的忏悔者》（"The Little Penitent"）、《小溪》（"The Brooklet"），还有《多莉的葬礼》（"Dolly's Funeral"）——弹得珠圆玉润，感情充沛。学生是西西（Sissie）、玛丽·霍利（Mary Holly）、斯图伊·格兰特（Stewy Grant）家的小男孩，还有邮政局长的女儿泰勒小姐（Miss Taylor）；而上课的大半光阴，都消磨在暖融融的闲谈里：讲讲她的烦恼，端出刚出炉的小甜饼请人尝，自己也吃上一两块，要不就领着学生进花园，去看玛吉的东方罂粟。

===Original===
“Music seems to mean so much to me!” Lily often said, and she was a trifle complacent at not being able to listen without tears to “Ten thousand times ten thousand,” that had been sung at Mamma’s funeral. “It’s funny, music always makes me feel like praying.” But everything made Lily feel like praying. Her bedtime prayers were so long and elaborate that, over and over again, she fell asleep in the midst of them, waking up later cold and stiff and drenched with moonlight. She had become very High Church, and while she hadn’t quite the courage to call Mr. Nelson “Father Nelson,” especially as he had a large family, her genuflections, and crossings, and flopping down on her knees in the middle of the Nicene Creed, provided much material for conversation at the Sunday dinner tables of the rest of the congregation. “Regular Roman Catholic!” was the general opinion. It was a dreadful nuisance having to have an egg or sardines for her on Fridays, and Maggie was always forgetting. Once they had had chicken salad for Friday supper, and Lily hadn’t been able to keep from crying.

===Chinese===
「音乐对我来说，意味有多深哪！」莉莉常说；连《万万千千》（"Ten thousand times ten thousand"）——妈妈葬礼上唱过的那首——她都不能无泪听完，还为此隐隐自得。「真奇怪，音乐总叫我觉得想做祷告。」其实什么事都叫莉莉觉得想做祷告。她的睡前祷告又长又讲究，多少回念着念着就睡着了，待到醒来，浑身冰冷僵硬，浸透了一身月光。她变得十分尊崇高教会派（High Church），虽然还没勇气管尼尔森先生（Mr. Nelson）叫「尼尔森神父」——何况他家儿女一大帮——可她那套屈膝礼、画十字、诵《尼西亚信经》诵到一半扑通跪倒的做派，为会众里其余人家的礼拜日餐桌提供了不少谈资。「十足的罗马天主教徒！」是众口一词的评断。每逢星期五得单给她备一个鸡蛋或几条沙丁鱼，烦煞人，玛吉又老是忘。有一回星期五晚饭上了鸡肉沙拉，莉莉忍了又忍，到底还是哭了。

===Original===
She thought of Lord Jesus so much, Gentle Jesus, who had loved her﻿—and all mankind, of course﻿—so much that he had died for her; and thinking of him her blue eyes swam with tears, her whole body seemed to melt into tears, tender and warm as spring rain.
“We must try to please our dear Lord Jesus Christ, children, mustn’t we?” she would say to her Sunday school class, and the little boys would look back at her solemnly from above ruffled collars and butterfly bows, the little girls from under hats like saucers holding cones of ice-cream, and agree politely; “Yes, Miss Lily.”

===Chinese===
她常常想念主耶稣，温柔的耶稣，他那样爱她——当然也爱全人类——爱到为她而死；一想到他，她的蓝眼睛便漾起泪水，整个身子仿佛都融成了泪水，温柔而暖热，如同春雨。

「我们必须努力讨亲爱的主耶稣基督的欢心，是不是，孩子们？」她总这样对她主日学的学生说。小男孩们从褶边衣领和蝴蝶领结上方一本正经地望着她，小女孩们从碟子似的、正好搁得下一个冰淇淋蛋卷的帽子底下望着她，客客气气地附和：「是的，莉莉小姐。」

===Original===
She did try so hard to please him, and live the spiritual life. Arranging the bouquets in Grandmother’s brass altar vases, she couldn’t help feeling sometimes, when there were strangers who had come early, that they might be whispering, as she made the flowers spray out or knelt before the altar, touched by the sunlight falling through the amber glass, “What a spiritual face! Who is she?”
Her Sunday school class! Her altar flowers! She was bursting with enthusiasm at first; and, when they got to be dreadful bores, she wouldn’t admit it even to herself. For if they were gone what would be left?

===Chinese===
她也当真拼命想讨他欢心，过灵性的生活。往祖母的黄铜圣坛花瓶里插花时，赶上有早到的生人在场，她有时便不由得想：正当她把花插得舒展喷薄、或跪在圣坛前、染上琥珀色玻璃透下的阳光时，她们或许正交头接耳：「好一张灵性的脸！她是谁？」

她的主日学班！她的圣坛花！起初她热忱得几乎要涨破胸怀；等这些变成叫人头疼的苦差，她连对自己都不肯承认。因为倘若没了这些，还能剩下什么呢？

===Original===
For a little while she thought Mr. Marshall would be left. She met him at Hessie Farley’s Valentine euchre party, in a romantic setting of red cardboard hearts, heart-shaped sandwiches, heart-shaped cakes with beetroot-reddened icing, and large crimson hearts of geranium blossoms. Mrs. Farley talked for a year about how much trouble it had all been. He was a rather wooden gentleman, but Lily garlanded him with the vine leaves of her imagination, and at parting he humorously offered her his scorecard heart, and asked if he might call some Saturday evening.
“Most evenings my rule is early to bed﻿—‘early to bed, early to rise,’ you know! But on Sundays I sleep an hour later in the morning, so I sit up an hour later on Saturday nights,” he explained.
So Lily, all in a flutter, took to running across the road to see old Mrs. Clark on Saturday evenings, hoping that Mr. Marshall would come to call.

===Chinese===
有那么一小阵子，她以为剩下的会是马歇尔先生（Mr. Marshall）。她是在赫西·法利家的情人节尤克牌会上遇见他的：红纸板剪的心、心形三明治、甜菜根汁把糖霜染红的心形蛋糕、天竺葵花簇成的大红心，一派旖旎光景。法利太太为这些张罗费了多少事，事后足足念叨了一年。他是位相当木讷的先生，可莉莉用想象的藤叶给他戴上了花环；临别时他诙谐地把自己那枚心形计分牌奉赠给她，又问可否哪个星期六晚上登门拜访。

「平日我恪守早睡之规——『早睡早起』，您是知道的！不过星期天早上我多睡一个钟头，所以星期六晚上便多坐一个钟头。」他解释道。

于是莉莉心旌摇曳，每逢星期六晚上便惯常跑过马路去看老克拉克太太（Mrs. Clark），只盼马歇尔先生前来拜访。

===Original===
“*Good evening, Miss Campion. Is Miss Lily in?*”
“*Good evening, Mr. Marshall. Excuse me a moment and I’ll call my sister﻿—she’s just across the road. She goes so often to see a poor old neighbor.*”
*And Mr. Marshall would think reverently*, “*Sweet Saint Charity!*”

===Chinese===
「*晚上好，坎皮恩小姐。莉莉小姐在家吗？*」

「*晚上好，马歇尔先生。请容我片刻，我去唤舍妹——她就在马路对面。她常去看望一位贫苦的老婆婆。*」

*而马歇尔先生便会肃然心想*：「*甜美的圣爱德！*」

===Original===
She tried to open doors into a world of wonders for Mrs. Clark.
“You know in India there’s a wonderful building called the Taj Mahal, all carved out of marble so that it looks as fine as lace, that an Indian rajah built in memory of his wife.”
“Patience guide me! Squaws they call them, don’t they? My nephew Will’s wife sent me a picture last week from the World’s Fair, and it said on it ‘Chief Big Crow and his squaw Little Cloud.’ ”
“But this is a different kind of Indian, Mrs. Clark﻿—Indians that live in *India*, way across the ocean, and ride around on elephants﻿—”
“Well, different kind or not, I wouldn’t trust ’em. Scalp you as soon as look at you, I’ve always heard tell.”

===Chinese===
她努力为克拉克太太推开一扇扇通往奇境的门。

「您知道吗，印度有一座奇妙的建筑，叫泰姬陵（Taj Mahal），通体用大理石雕成，精致得赛过蕾丝，是一位印度土王为纪念亡妻建造的。」

「老天爷指引我！他们管那些女人叫『婆娘』，是不是？我侄子威尔的媳妇上礼拜从世界博览会给我寄来一张画片，上头写着『大乌鸦酋长和他的婆娘小云』。」

「可这是另一种印第安人呀，克拉克太太——住在*印度*的印度人，在大洋那一头，出门骑着大象——」

「哼，不管是哪一种，反正我信不过他们。一照面就剥你的头皮，我一直是这么听人说的。」

===Original===
And all the time Lily was listening for the click of the gate, steps on the path, Maggie’s breathless voice saying, “Lily! Mr. Marshall’s over at the house!” But he never came, and it just let her in for a tiresome custom, for what could she do when Mrs. Clark greeted her with, “I was just settin’ here in the dark feelin’ so lonesome, and hopin’ my uttermost best that you’d come!”
Every Saturday night Lily carried a bunch of flowers to Mrs. Clark, so that Mr. Clark’s grave, conveniently next to the Methodist Church, might be admired on Sunday.
