The house grew friendly again. “Tickticktick!” said the clock, brisk and cheerful. She could hear the mice scratching in the walls﻿—it sounded as if they were having fun together. The boiling chocolate made a thick bubbling sound and smelt delicious.

“ ‘Boil till it forms in a soft ball in cold water,’ ” she said aloud. Good little soft balls! She ate five or six of them before she suddenly remembered that it was time to pour the candy out and she had forgotten to butter the soup plates.

It turned nice and hard﻿—a pleasant surprise, so often her candy had to be eaten with a spoon. She marked it off into squares, sampling a good many. Fudge or caramels? She couldn’t make up her mind. She filled a dish and took it in to the living-room, where she had a game of her favorite Tiddley-winks﻿—another game﻿—another. She wasn’t quite sure about playing Tiddley-winks on Sunday, but she was so lonely in the evening without Maggie, she had to do something. Just *one* more game. And as she played her hand strayed from the dish to her mouth.

===Chinese===

> 「 ‘耶稣，温柔的牧人，求你垂听；

> 今夜赐福你的小羊；

> 黑暗中求你与我相亲相近——’ 」

屋子重新变得亲热起来。「滴答滴答！」时钟说，又爽利，又欢畅。她听得见墙里老鼠窸窸窣窣——听着倒像它们正一处玩得快活。滚沸的巧克力咕嘟咕嘟冒着稠密的泡，香喷喷的。

「 ‘熬到放进冷水里能团成软球，’ 」她大声念道。乖巧的小软球！她一连吃了五六个，才猛然想起该把糖倒出来了，而汤盘竟忘了抹油。

糖凝得又好又硬——真是桩叫人欢喜的意外，往常她的糖，多半得用汤匙舀着吃。她把它划成一小方一小方，一路试吃了好些。算法奇糖呢，还是算焦糖？她拿不定主意。她装了满满一碟，端进客厅，在那儿玩起心爱的弹码游戏（Tiddley-winks）——一局，又一局，又一局。星期天玩弹码游戏到底妥不妥，她心里也不大有底，可是晚上没有了玛吉，她实在孤单，总得做点什么。只*再*来一局。玩着玩着，她那只手便不知不觉从碟子边游到了嘴边。


===Original===

When Victor came in, wrapped in cold March air, the dish was empty. She couldn’t believe it!

“Oh, *Victor*! I made them all for you﻿—for a surprise!”

She could have cried, if she hadn’t remembered her other surprise.

“Guess what!”

“What?”

“Well﻿—Lucy Hawthorn’s back in New York﻿—oh, I humbly beg her pardon, I should have said Her Royal Highness, the Countess of de la Villeblanche﻿—”

===Chinese===

维克托进门来的时候，裹着一身三月的寒气，那碟糖已经空了。她简直不能相信！

「哎呀，*维克托*！我全是做给你吃的呀——想给你个惊喜！」

若不是还惦着另一桩惊喜，她真要哭出来了。

「你猜猜！」

「猜什么？」

「这个嘛——露西·霍桑回纽约啦——哦，我谦卑地请她恕罪，本该称她一声德·拉·维尔布朗什伯爵夫人殿下——」

---

===Original===

Lucy home again! The little feet that had carried her so far were bringing her back to him. And he saw himself receiving into his arms that piteous and fragile figure, heard her say brokenly:

“Victor﻿—at last﻿—”

Should he write to her? He tore up three letters, and then wrote one light in tone but with hidden depths, he really did think, and posted it in a perfect panic. And back came a note from Lucy, so charming that it sounded as if she had come back to America just for his sake.

He decided to go to New York to see her. He could go in the morning and come back after supper, and then he needn’t tell Lily anything about it.

Would she be changed? But people didn’t change really. He was the same Victor who had come to New York to see Lucy years ago﻿—he felt as young, if he admitted the truth, he felt as nervous. He walked from the Pennsylvania Station to the Ritz, dreamily escaping death among the taxicabs. Coming for a moment out of his absorption in the queer feeling growing in the pit of his stomach, he noticed a man with a tray of flowers for sale, among them a few little bunches of lilies-of-the-valley with leaves as tender and pale a green as butterfly wings. Lilies-of-the-valley. “Lucy’s flowers.” Their sweetness would build a bridge across the years, they would say the things he could not. He bought a bunch and slipped it in its screw of waxed paper into his overcoat pocket.

===Chinese===

露西又回家来了！当年载着她走出那么远的一双小脚，如今正把她带回他身边。他仿佛看见自己张开双臂，把那可怜而纤弱的人影接进怀里，听见她哽哽咽咽地说：

「维克托——总算——」

他该不该给她写信呢？他撕了三封，才写成一封——语气轻浅，底下却藏着深意，他自个儿也当真这么觉得——寄出去时，慌得六神无主。露西那边回了张短笺，写得那样妩媚，听着倒仿佛她回美国来，单单就是为了他。

他决定上纽约去看她。可以一早动身，吃过晚饭就回来，这样对莉莉便一个字也不必提。

她会变样么？可人其实是不会变的。他还是多年前上纽约看露西的那个维克托——他觉得自己一样年轻，说句实话，也一样紧张。他从宾夕法尼亚车站（Pennsylvania Station）步行到里兹饭店（the Ritz），恍恍惚惚地在出租汽车阵里躲过一场场死劫。有一刻，他从心窝底下那阵越结越古怪的感觉里回过神来，看见一个托着卖花盘子的男人，盘里有好几小束铃兰，叶片的绿又嫩又浅，像蝴蝶翅膀。铃兰。「露西的花。」它们的清香会在逝去的年华上架起一座桥，替他说出他说不出口的话。他买了一束，连同那一小卷蜡纸，一起塞进大衣口袋。


===Original===

And then he was in Lucy’s apartment at the Ritz, and Lucy herself was holding his hand between hers. Lucy? But where was Lucy? This fat old woman with her powered face and reddened mouth and her queer coppery hair, wearing no jewels but the three strands of great pearls laid on the shelf of her bosom, and draped in heaviest crêpe, mourning as one who eats caviar, oysters, *sole marguery*, mourns because Christ died upon a Friday, what had she done with Lucy? Was his slim white dryad really there, imprisoned in this tree with its roots in the earth?

“Victor! After all these years﻿—no, dreadful man, don’t dare to say how many! My daughter Madame de Griche﻿—Mrs. Portal, Mrs. Lee, General Scudworth﻿—my grandson Marcel﻿—Monsieur Campion is a dear and old friend of Grand’maman’s, *p’tit*﻿—my little granddaughter Lucie. Tea! Tea! Ring for tea, Colette, *chérie*. Tell them heaps of *confitures*! Don’t sit on Galette or Chrysanthème, anyone﻿—”

Madame de Griche, ugly and chic, her black hair cut like a boy’s, the pearls in her ears and the cigarette drooping from her lacquer-red mouth startlingly white against her yellow skin, was pouring the tea. There were dishes heaped with the ruby and topaz of jam and marmalade, mauve tin boxes from Sherry’s stood open, disclosing chocolates and small rich cakes, the room was full of flowers wilting a little in the steam heat﻿—white lilac really swooning, blue and yellow iris, powdery-sweet mimosa. The two silky, fat toy dogs, like lumps of half-melted toffee, were yapping and being fed chocolates by Lucy, whose stern little granddaughter was chiding her. “*Non, non, Grand’maman, c’est méchant! Va-tu, Chrysanthème! Chrysanthème! Galette! Taissez-vous, mauvaises chiens!*” Boxes were arriving, the telephone ringing, Marcel’s high voice was crying into it, “*Alloh, Alloh!*” Old friends rushed in and were embraced.

“*Le téléphone, Maman!*”

Lucy took the teapot. “How do you take your tea, Victor?”

“Two lumps and cream, please.”

So she gave him lemon, saying, “Now we must have a long, long talk about the dear old days!” But things kept happening.

===Chinese===

转眼间，他已经在里兹饭店露西的套房里，露西本人正把他的手夹在她两手当中。露西？可露西在哪儿呢？眼前这个满脸扑粉、嘴唇涂红、顶着一头古怪铜色头发的胖老太太，除了搁在胸脯那道搁架上的三串大珍珠，什么首饰也不戴，通身裹着最重的黑绉纱——那服丧的架势，就像吃着鱼子酱、牡蛎和*马尔盖里鳎鱼*（sole marguery）的人，只因为基督在星期五受难而举哀——她把露西怎么处置了？他那苗条雪白的树中仙子，当真还在这里，囚在这棵根须扎进泥土的树里么？

「维克托！隔了这么多年——不，狠心的男人，可别敢说出是多少年！我女儿德·格里什夫人（Madame de Griche）——波塔尔太太（Mrs. Portal）、李太太（Mrs. Lee）、斯卡德沃思将军（General Scudworth）——我的外孙马塞尔（Marcel）——坎皮恩先生是外婆顶亲顶老的朋友呢，*小家伙*（p'tit）——我的小外孙女露茜（Lucie）。茶！茶！按铃上茶，科莱特（Colette），*亲爱的*（chérie）。叫他们多多的上*果酱*（confitures）！谁也不许坐在加莱特和克里桑黛姆（Galette、Chrysanthème）身上——」

德·格里什夫人人长得丑，派头却时兴，黑头发剪得像男孩子，耳上的珍珠、漆红嘴角斜叼着的香烟，衬着黄黄的皮肤，白得扎眼，这会儿正张罗着斟茶。一盘盘果酱、橘子酱，堆得像红宝石、黄玉；谢里糕饼店（Sherry's）的淡紫色铁皮盒开着口，露出巧克力和油润的小蛋糕；满屋子都是花，在蒸汽暖气里微微打着蔫——白丁香简直真要晕过去，蓝的、黄的鸢尾，粉扑扑甜香的金合欢。两只毛缎般肥滚滚的哈巴狗，像两块半化的太妃糖，一面汪汪叫，一面由露西喂着巧克力，她那板着小脸的外孙女正在数落她：「*不，不，外婆，这样使坏可不行！去，克里桑黛姆！克里桑黛姆！加莱特！住嘴，两只坏狗！*」盒子接二连三地送到，电话铃响个不停，马塞尔尖细的嗓门冲着话筒喊：「*喂，喂！*」老朋友们一阵风似的闯进来，挨个儿受了拥抱。

「*电话，妈妈！*」

露西拎起茶壶。「你的茶怎么调，维克托？」

「请放两块方糖，兑点奶油。」

于是她给了他柠檬，口里说道：「这会儿咱们可得好好、好好地聊聊亲爱的老日子啦！」可事情总是一桩接一桩。


===Original===

And then his body followed his spirit that had long ago hurried away towards home. The March air flowed cool and delicious over his hot face, he let his mouth relax from its stretch of polite and nervous smile. He could catch the six o’clock train, he would be home before ten!

He had let his bunch of lilies-of-the-valley stay crushed in his pocket. They had been too gentle. And he thought sadly, complacently:

“Poor Lucy, how fat she has grown﻿—and how old!”

===Chinese===

后来，他的身子这才追上他那早已急急赶回家去的心。三月的空气凉凉地、舒舒服服地流过他滚烫的脸，他让嘴从那一长程客气而紧张的微笑里松懈下来。六点钟的火车赶得上，十点以前就能到家了！

他任凭那束铃兰一直压瘪在口袋里。它们实在太温婉了。他悲哀地、却又心满意足地想：

「可怜的露西，她变得多胖了啊——也多老了呀！」

## XXXIII / XXXIII

===Original===

Lily didn’t really mind getting old. Not that sixty-six was old, but it certainly wasn’t young. Sometimes, when the house was cold and she shivered in spite of her shabby blue sweater, or when her mayonnaise “went back on her,” or she couldn’t put off going to the dentist any longer, she was sad. But there were market days when she stayed in Wilmington and had a chocolate sundae at Reynold’s for lunch, and bought a quarter of a pound of bitter-chocolate peppermints to eat at the movies. There were happy nights of early to bed, with a hot-water bag and a moving picture magazine﻿—beautiful Mr. Valentino in a sash and a broad-brimmed hat, Barbara La Marr’s velvet mouth, and stories about how much those nice Talmadge girls loved their mother, the innocence of darling little Jackie Coogan, and what a good husband Mr. Menjou was in spite of his lifted eyebrows. “How good everybody is, when you really *know*,” thought Lily. And there were the stars’ bedrooms﻿—she pored over every detail. Dolls in hoopskirts that hatched out telephones, cushions trimmed with bunches of silk fruit﻿—rather knobby for the head? Still, as they were mostly strewn about the floor, perhaps you weren’t meant to put your head down on them.

And going to the post office was exciting﻿—not many letters, but she sent for so many things that there was almost always mail for her. Samples of perfumes, of cold cream, of note paper, “Orchid” and “Willow-Green” and “Straw,” catalogues of Dutch bulbs and peasant furniture, booklets about trips around the world.

And there was always church, the changing of the colored hangings, flowers on the altar, the luxurious warm bath of the soul of her prayers.

===Chinese===

莉莉其实并不真把变老放在心上。倒不是说六十六岁就算老，可也断乎说不上年轻了。有时候，屋里冷，她尽管套着那件寒酸的蓝毛衣还是直打哆嗦；有时候，她的蛋黄酱「半途倒戈」；有时候，看牙医这件事再也没法往后拖了——她便不免难过。可也有赶集的日子，她留在威尔明顿城里，在雷诺兹糖果店吃一客巧克力圣代当午饭，再买上四分之一磅苦巧克力薄荷糖，留着看电影时吃。也有早早安寝的快活夜晚，怀里揣着热水袋，捧一本电影杂志——系着饰带、戴着宽檐帽的俊美的瓦伦蒂诺先生（Mr. Valentino），芭芭拉·拉·马尔（Barbara La Marr）那张天鹅绒似的嘴，还有种种故事：讲塔尔梅奇姐妹（the Talmadge girls）那两个好姑娘怎样深爱她们的母亲，讲小可爱杰基·库根（Jackie Coogan）多么天真烂漫，讲门茹先生（Mr. Menjou）眉毛尽管吊着，却是个多么好的丈夫。「只要你真正*了解*，人人都是多么好哇，」莉莉想。还有明星们的卧室——她一处细节一处细节地细细玩味。穿着旧式撑裙的玩偶会孵出电话机来，镶着一簇簇绢果的靠垫——拿来枕脑袋不是硌得慌么？不过它们多半东一只西一只摊在地板上，可见原本也没打算叫人把头搁上去。

还有上邮局，也是桩叫人兴奋的事——信倒没有几封，可她函购的东西实在不少，差不多回回都有她的邮件。香水的、冷霜的、信纸的样品，什么「兰花紫」啦「柳绿」啦「麦秆黄」啦，荷兰球茎花卉的目录、乡村风味家具的目录，还有讲环球旅行的册子。

还有教堂，那是永远去不厌的——彩色帐幔随节令更换，圣坛上供着鲜花，她的祈祷，便是灵魂泡的一场奢华暖浴。


===Original===

But Victor hated growing old, and fought against it, jumping off cars before they stopped, and walking jauntily away, refusing to wear an overcoat when he was blue with cold﻿—*wanting* to be young.

“I will do exercises and take cold plunges,” he decided, not for the first time. “That’s the way for a fellow to keep young.” And morning after morning, Lily heard him puffing, panting, granting﻿—thump﻿—thump﻿—thump﻿—heard the water roaring into the tub, and then the splash that meant most of it was leaping out again, hastily followed by Victor. She was dreadfully worried about the parlor ceiling.

“Wonderful the feeling of vitality it gives you,” he told the bored men at the office. “I get the greatest reaction out of an ice-cold plunge, just as cold as it comes, no lukewarm baths for yours truly, I leave those for the old fellows.” He would stop the most casual acquaintances to tell them about his exercises and cold baths. “Why don’t *you* try it, old man?”

But gradually they tapered off and stopped. He began to drink buttermilk. He had read somewhere that buttermilk would keep you young practically forever.

===Chinese===

维克托却恨变老，一心跟它对着干：街车还没停稳就往下跳，跳下来还要神气活现地扬长而去；冻得浑身发青也不肯穿大衣——他*巴望*着自己年轻。

「我要练体操，还要洗冷水浴，」他拿定了主意，这也不是头一遭了。「男人要保住年轻，就得这么办。」于是一个清早接一个清早，莉莉听得见他呼哧呼哧、气喘吁吁、哼哼唧唧——咚——咚——咚——听得见水轰隆隆灌进浴缸，接着哗啦一声大响——这一声意味着，大半缸水又蹦了出来，后头急急忙忙跟着一个维克托。她为客厅的天花板担足了心。

「这给了你多妙的一股子生命力呀，」他对办公室里那些听烦了的人们说。「冰水浴给我的反应最大，水来得有多冷就用多冷，本人在此声明概不洗温吞水，那玩意儿留给老头子们去享受吧。」连顶泛泛的点头之交他也要拦住，跟人家讲他的体操和冷水浴。「老兄，*你*怎么不来一套？」

可是渐渐地，这套把戏一天稀似一天，末了全停了。他喝起酪乳来了。他在什么地方读到过：酪乳能叫人青春不老，几乎永远年轻下去。


===Original===

His clothes were getting old, too. There was his fur-lined coat, with the fur collar, that he hadn’t realized wasn’t still something to be proud of, until one day someone said to him:

“You ought to give that coat back to the rat and let it finish it.”

It was as scrubby as that! So scrubby that a friend could think he was just wearing it to be funny. And he pretended he was. He thrust a hand into its breast, put out one foot, and declaimed:

“Methinks, you wot not that you address Hamfat, the famous tragedian!”

But his heart was crying.

There weren’t as many invitations as there used to be. People weren’t entertaining as much as they had entertained before the war, he told himself. But he went everywhere he was asked, and he paid a great many calls.

===Chinese===

他的衣着也见老了。就说那件毛皮衬里的大衣，连领子也是毛皮的，他一直不曾醒悟它早已算不得一件可夸之物，直到有一天有人对他说：

「你这件大衣，应该物归原主还给耗子，好让耗子把它接着织完。」

它竟寒碜到了这步田地！寒碜得叫朋友只当他穿着它是存心搞笑。他便索性装出真在搞笑的样子。他把一只手插进衣襟，一脚向前踏出，朗声念白道：

「量你不知，同你讲话的这位，乃是大名鼎鼎的悲剧泰斗哈姆法特（Hamfat）！」

可他心里在哭。

请柬也不似从前那么多了。如今人们不像战前那样兴师动众地请客了，他这样宽慰自己。不过凡有相请，他无处不到，还登门拜了许多客。

---

===Original===

Four members of Dorothy Spear’s house party were playing mah-jongg at the top of their lungs, four more were dancing to “It Ain’t Goin’ to Rain No More,” played by the gramophone. Dorothy herself lay stretched on the sofa, slender legs in flesh-colored silk stockings showing to the knees, waving a cigarette in a long holder at Gregory Hart to emphasize a few remarks on suppressed desires.

“Love your cigarette holder, Dot!” Bunny Tempest called as she foxtrotted past.

“You ought to see Edwina’s﻿—hers is so long she has to have a flute case to carry it in. Throw us another Lucky, Tommy!”

Two more friends appeared in the doorway, the girl slender and straight as a pencil, her painted baby face framed in a tight cloche and a great roll of fur collar, the boy voluminous in raccoon coat, with much pale blue muffler.

===Chinese===

多萝西·斯皮尔（Dorothy Spear）家乡居聚会的客人里，四位正扯开嗓子打麻将，另有四位正和着留声机里放的《再也不会下雨了》（It Ain’t Goin’ to Rain No More）跳舞。多萝西本人直挺挺躺在沙发上，一双肉色丝袜裹着的纤腿直露到膝盖，朝格雷戈里·哈特（Gregory Hart）挥着长烟嘴上的香烟，给她那几句论「被压抑的欲望」的话加重语气。

「你这烟嘴儿真叫人爱，多特！」邦妮·坦佩斯特（Bunny Tempest）跳着狐步舞打旁边过，扬声喊道。

「你该瞧瞧埃德温娜的那支才好——那支长得只好拿笛子盒来装。再扔给我们一包『幸运牌』，汤米！」

门口又进来两位朋友：姑娘细溜笔直像支铅笔，一张搽了脂粉的娃娃脸，嵌在绷紧的钟形帽和大圈毛皮领子里；小伙子裹着臃肿的浣熊皮大衣，淡蓝色的围巾缠了老大一堆。


===Original===

“Hello, you dumbbells!”

“Hello, yourselves! Don’t you look Ritzy? Take off a few clothes﻿—oh, my *dear*, what a *per*fect boyish bob! Oh, it looks simply *darling*, I’m *crazy* about it!”

“She looks like a drowned kitten.”

“Oh, ankle along, you Victorian! Don’t pay any attention to him, Mariette, if he *is* your fiasco!”

===Chinese===

「你们好哇，一对笨蛋！」

「你们自己才好呢！瞧这通身的气派，多阔气！脱几件衣裳吧——哦，我*亲爱的*，这副男孩子式的短发剪得多*完*美呀！哦，简直*可爱*死了，我*迷*死它了！」

「她活像只落水的小猫。」

「哦，走你的吧，你这维多利亚的老古董！别搭理他，玛丽埃特（Mariette），哪怕他*当真*是你那位未婚夫！」


===Original===

Mrs. Spear paused at the door on the way upstairs for a nap, and said resignedly:

“Dorothy, your legs.”

“Nice, aren’t they?”

“Pull down your skirt, darling.”

“Listen to my *precious* mother! She’s a scream, boys and girls. Don’t you think she’s a *scream*? You are, you know, Ma!”

“Play something else﻿—‘Doo Wacky Doo’ or something.”

“Come and dance, Dot.”

===Chinese===

斯皮尔太太上楼去打个盹，路过门口停了停，无可奈何地说：

「多萝西，你的腿。」

「好看，不是吗？」

「把裙子放下来些，亲爱的。」

「听听我这*宝贝*母亲！她可真是笑死人，姑娘们小伙子们。你们说她是不是笑死人？您就是，妈，您自己知道！」

「换一张别的片子吧——《杜瓦奇杜》（Doo Wacky Doo）什么的。」

「来跳个舞吧，多特。」


===Original===

The hurrying excitement of the music shook them﻿—a queer stab went through her as Gregory held her closer. She was almost too breathless to say mockingly:

“Oh, you passionate orchid-crusher!”

“Look at King Tut coming up the drive!” someone called from the window seat, and someone else cried, “What is it? I *ask* you, what *is* it?”

===Chinese===

音乐那催人的兴奋撼动着他们——格雷戈里把她搂得更紧时，一阵古怪的刺痛扎过她心头。她喘得几乎透不过气来，还是勉强嘲弄了一句：

「哦，你这蹂躏兰花的热情郎！」

「瞧，图坦卡蒙顺着车道过来啦！」窗座那边有人喊；又有一个叫道：「这是什么呀？我*问*你们，这到底*是*什么呀？」


===Original===

Dorothy looked over their heads. Mr. Victor Campion in his high silk hat, out making Sunday afternoon calls.

“Oh, my dears, he’s our hardy perennial bachelor. Stop the music﻿—grab Thompson, someone, before he gets to the door﻿—if we once let him in he’ll stay to tea and Lord knows how much longer. Thompson﻿—Thompson﻿—not at home﻿—”

She watched him turn away, and an unexpected pang of pity stirred in her heart.

“This used to be his home﻿—it must be queer to have us in it. He and three old maid sisters used to live here. One of them went dippy from sex repression﻿—just like what we were talking about, Greg. Too bad they didn’t have her psyched. There’s only one left, and she certainly is the hole in the doughnut. Well﻿—might as well turn on the music.”

===Chinese===

多萝西越过众人的头顶望出去。维克托·坎皮恩先生，戴着高顶丝帽，正出门来做星期天下午的拜客。

「哦，我亲爱的们，他就是咱们那位耐寒的多年生单身汉。把音乐停了——哪位去截住汤普森（Thompson），赶在他走到门口之前——一旦让他进了门，他就要留下来吃茶点，天知道还要待到什么时候。汤普森——汤普森——就说不在家——」

她望着他转身走开，心里意外地掠过一阵怜惜的刺痛。

「这宅子从前就是他的家——如今换了我们住着，他心里必定怪别扭的。他从前和三个当老姑娘的妹妹住在这儿。有一个竟因为性压抑，脑子憋出了毛病——正跟咱们方才谈的一样，格雷格。可惜没请人给她做过精神分析。如今只剩下一个了，她真是甜甜圈正当中那个洞。好啦——音乐还是照旧开起来吧。」


===Original===

Victor walked down the drive. Had he heard smothered giggling when the door was opened? There was the beech tree they used to play under, Maggie and May and Lily and he. Should he make some more calls? No, he was tired, and it was chilly and late. There was snow in that sky, though a cold pink colored the west. Sunset. Soon it would be dark.

Lily was watching for him at the window, standing over the register, her skirts ballooning with heat. She ran to the door to let him in.

“Oh, Victor, you did look so nice coming along the road! I always think there’s no one in the world looks as nice in a silk hat as you!”

===Chinese===

维克托顺着车道走下去。方才门开的那会儿，他可曾听见捂住的咯咯笑声？那棵山毛榉还在——他们从前常在树下玩耍，玛吉、梅、莉莉和他。还要再拜几家么？不了，他累了，天又冷，时候也晚。那片天空里是要落雪的，虽然西天染着一抹冷冷的粉红。日落了。转眼就要天黑了。

莉莉正守在窗口望他，人站在暖气风口上，裙子被热气吹得鼓鼓的。她奔到门口去放他进来。

「哦，维克托，你顺着大路走过来的样子真好看！我一向觉得，这世上把丝帽戴得像你这样好看的，再没有第二个人！」
